# Decisions

- 2026-10-13 HiGHS core: highspy 1.15.1 → 1.15.1, highs-js 1.15.3 → 1.15.1; parity tests (Phase 4) decide whether a patch-level mismatch matters. Both builds also report source revision 04024d7 (highspy `Highs.githash()`, highs-js `highs.version.gitHash`), so there is no mismatch today.
- 2026-10-14 Presolve gate (Phase 2; HiGHS 1.15.1, revision 04024d7; reports/bench-2026-10-14.md): 500 scenarios drawn uniformly from the grid (all tiers, races, trios, roles and setups, both buff sets, wiki and strict-proxy pools) were solved with presolve off and on: 0 objective mismatches (the largest relative difference is 0), 0 presolve runs that did not end optimal, 0 errors; the canonical solve agreed too on the 100 scenarios that ran it. The existing exactness suites (hand-checked models, brute force, solver wrapper, pruning: 354 tests) also pass with presolve on. Presolve stays OFF anyway: on this formulation it runs at 0.71x the speed of presolve off for the plain solve and 0.49x with the tie stage (the 1.44x seen on the earlier model does not hold), so it buys nothing. `_highs.OPTIONS["presolve"]` stays "off"; the 5,000-scenario gate is a later CI step (`python -m trioforge.bench --gate-only --sample 5000`, which exits 1 on a mismatch).
- 2026-10-14 Precompute scope (reports/bench-2026-10-14.md): the full grid of 2,825,460 scenarios needs about 681 CPU hours with the tie stage and the wiki-first cut (95% interval 581 to 794; 879 with no cuts; 295 for the first solve alone), measured as CPU seconds on a 24-thread machine with every thread busy. That is above the 300-hour trigger, so **tiers +0, +4 and +10 are precomputed (about 189 CPU hours, 2.4 hours of wall time on 80 vCPUs) and the other eight tiers solve in the browser**. The decision does not hang on the machine (measured alone it took 0.77x the hours, still about 520), on the wiki-first share (even if every strict solve were skipped it is about 610) or on presolve (above); the tie stage decides it, since the first solve alone would be 295. Basis: 7,884 coverage scenarios (61 trios of all five role-set types, with 13 Monk trios, 18 trios that mix WIS and INT mana and 5 all-casters; races ERU, HUM and OGR; tiers 0, 4 and 10; own and group buffs; every role and weapon setup) plus 3,000 uniform scenarios of the whole grid, each also run as a strict-proxy twin (the public catalog has no strict items; the proxy is a fixed 44% of the wiki items). Mean CPU seconds per scenario: first solve 0.48 (p50 0.20, p95 1.6, max 3.9); solve with the tie stage 1.37 (p50 0.44, p95 6.3, max 12.6); building the model 0.20. The 74% of strict solves that wiki-first skips was measured on the earlier model, not here. Browser proxy: highs-js 1.15.1 solves 20 benchmark models in a median of 107 ms and p95 of 949 ms on the desktop, about 430 ms and 3.8 s at 4x slower: within the phone budget (median under 1 s, p95 under 5 s).

- 2026-10-14 Seen flags in releases and in CI: the release notes publish a random snapshot id, drawn fresh for each release and not derived from the flags, and never a hash of the flags file or of a build made with it (a hash of a short list of item keys can be checked against guesses, and it dates every change to the list). No release-notes generator exists yet, so this is the rule for the workflow that will be written. The flags reach a build only as the secret `TRIOFORGE_SEEN_FLAGS` of a protected environment: the workflow writes it to `$RUNNER_TEMP` and never into the workspace, never uploads anything under `build/catalog-with-seen/`, and never gives the secret to the pull-request job.
- 2026-10-14 EQL Tools cross-check (reports/eqltools-diff-2026-10-14.md; EQL Tools gear data of 2026-10-11, CC BY-SA 4.0): 6,803 items match by name. The stats and resists agree except for wiki typos and newer page revisions (2 AC, 1 weight). The 37 class and 25 slot differences are EQL Tools' readings of wrapped Class lines and of instruments; ours follow the pages. The era disagreements are all explained by deliberate rules of the evidence ladder:
  - The 1,120 items we hide and EQL Tools calls in era are cultural-container recipes (637, a player report), disputed items kept out of default kits (283), items with no known live source (273) and items the wiki marks as not in EQL (173), plus small groups (out-of-era ingredients, research removals, zones not in EQL).
  - The 8 items we keep at wiki and EQL Tools calls out of era each have a live source (a drop in a live zone, a quest or a vendor), which the ladder ranks above era tags.
  - "Only in EQL Tools" is mostly wiki disambiguation titles (for example the bow pages ending in "(Hemp)"); our catalog keeps the page title as the item name. The browser's dump matcher strips the disambiguator (Phase 4, `match_name`).
  Nothing in the report is unexplained.
- 2026-10-14 Canonical kits are now placed by a rule that looks at the items alone (the worn items outside the hands go by key and tier into the earliest slot group that leaves room for the rest). With presolve off and on, 100 of 100 canonical kits match place for place (85 of 100 before that rule), so the precomputed kits do not depend on solver internals that differ between engines.
- 2026-10-14 An empty race list in the catalog means the page says "Race: NONE": no race can wear the item, and it is not for best-in-slot. (An earlier ruling that read `[]` as "any race" was reversed after the pages were checked: all 271 such lists come from that line.)
- 2026-10-14 `npm audit` findings accepted for now (dev tools only): `npm audit` reports 3 high findings, all one advisory (GHSA-wq5f-xc86-pv6w, CVE-2026-96889, a vulnerability in the librsvg that `sharp` carries) in `sharp` below 0.35.5, reached through the pinned wrangler 4.144.0 (wrangler, then miniflare, then `sharp` 0.35.4). Nothing from it ships in the site: wrangler is a dev dependency that serves the built static files for the browser tests and the local preview, and the image code of `sharp` is not used by that. The fix is wrangler 4.151.0 or later (`npm audit` offers 4.151.0, a minor bump). The finding is accepted until the fixed release is at least 14 days old: 4.151.0 came out on 2026-10-14, so **wrangler is bumped to 4.151.0 or later on or after 2026-10-28**, in `package.json` and `package-lock.json` together, with `npm audit` and the browser tests run again, and this entry is then closed.
- 2026-10-14 One attribution text: the footer of every page, the sources page, LICENSE-DATA and every data file of the site say the same words, which credit the wiki the items come from and EQL Tools, whose quest data gives the zones of the quest items (`build.ATTRIBUTION`; the footer links the names, and a test puts the pieces back together to the letter). The catalog's own attribution line in `sources-manifest.json` is no longer printed beside it: two wordings of one credit were what a reader saw before. The licence sentence of the footer is the one LICENSE-DATA ends with (the data is CC BY-SA 4.0 except the game facts read from Daybreak's files, which are Daybreak's), and every data file the page fetches carries `release`, `license` and `attribution`.
- 2026-10-14 The report links wait for the project's own GitHub account: an indexable build needs `--owner NAME`, a preview needs none and then links to no repository ("Reports open when the project is public"), and the word OWNER, which the repository files used for the account, is refused in every build because it is a real account of someone else. The issue chooser has no link to the security policy for the same reason; it is added with the account's name when the repository is public. An indexable build also refuses results of a narrowed precompute run (marked partial), which can only make a preview that says so on every page.

- 2026-10-14 Budget of a data file: a data file of the site (the four shards of a trio, a runner-ups file, the robust file) may be 200 KB raw and 50 KB gzip, and the build refuses a bigger one (`build.LIMITS`, which also holds the other budgets: 60 KB gzip for the first load of a trio page, 30 KB gzip of that in script, 100 KB of script in all). The first switch of a visitor costs one shard on top of the first load, so the most a phone fetches before it shows another view than the baked one is 60 KB plus 50 KB gzip.
- 2026-10-14 Race and Tier are native selects, not radio groups: the switches were designed as radio groups with labels, and the role (3 choices), the weapons, the buffs and the evidence level are. Race has 15 options and tier has 11 options, which as radio buttons would fill several screens of a phone; a select is a native, labelled control that every screen reader and every keyboard handles, and the browser tests drive both with the keyboard. The races are in two option groups, the ones a character can start as and the ones that need an unlock.
- 2026-10-14 Permissions-Policy: the header switches off 44 features in one line, in alphabetical order: every sensor, device, capture, credential and payment interface of the browser, and the advertising interfaces (Topics, attribution reporting, the old cohort id, Protected Audience, Shared Storage, Private Aggregation). A test pins the list, and that the line stays within the 2,000 characters Cloudflare Pages reads of a line of `_headers`. Left out on purpose: clipboard-write and web-share (the copy and share buttons of the kit links in the next phase may need them) and cross-origin-isolated (a solver with threads in the browser may). The Chromium of the browser tests is built without the Privacy Sandbox and warns that six of the names are unrecognized (Chrome knows them); the watcher of the browser tests lets exactly those six warnings pass, a test checks that, and a warning about any other name is still a problem of the page.
- 2026-10-14 The phone shows the answer first: below 600 px the switches fold behind one line that says what is shown (race, role, tier and the assumptions) and a Change button that opens them, so that "Wear this" and its first row are on the first screen of a 360 by 740 phone (a browser test measures it, under the banner of a preview). For the same reason the notes under the headline (the rules the best race is judged by, and what could change it) are in one closed expander, and the headline names its view itself, so that the claim is never read without it. Without script the switches are disabled (and on a phone not shown at all: there is nothing to open), and the note that says why comes before them.
- 2026-10-14 Scenario requests stay "not available yet" in the browser engine, and scenario parity waits for the JavaScript model builder: a request of the kind `scenario` (a trio, a level, owned items; the page sends no model) fails at once with `not-implemented`, before the visitor is asked and before anything is downloaded, and the worker answers the same if one reaches it. Until the builder lands the parity tests of the two readers judge model JSON v1 documents only (`fixtures/milp/`), since the page cannot yet make a model from a scenario; the scenario fields are still checked against the manifest and as plain data. When the builder is wired in, `SCENARIOS_READY` in `static/engine/client.js` turns true and a test of the two builders over the same scenarios comes with it.
- 2026-10-14 `kind` is a required part of every request to the browser engine: a request is `{v, kind, budgetMs, ...}` where `kind` is `model` (with a `model` in model JSON v1) or `scenario` (with a trio, a level and the optional scenario, owned, candidates and mode), and the reader refuses a request with no `kind`, with another one, or with a field that does not belong to its kind (each kind has its own list of fields). Nothing guesses the kind from the other fields, so what the page meant is what the worker does, and a new kind is added to the lists and to the tests on purpose.
- 2026-10-16 Canonical hands, which extends the entry of 2026-10-14 on canonical kits: the canonical kit now also fixes what stands in the hands. The earlier rule left the hands as the solver chose them, and highspy and highs-js chose differently for the same items at the same value (4 of the 533 canonical kits of the fixtures: upgrade-002 step 3, upgrade-005 step 2, pmb36-013 and brute-265). Of the arrangements of the worn items that the builder allows (the model has a column for the item in that hand, which is what the weapon setup, the slots and the class rules say; a two-hander leaves the off hand empty; a pair of one item needs two copies; whatever is left must still have a place outside the hands) and whose exact value equals the solution's (the evaluator, under the solution's buffs and the setup, assumed haste and Monk bins of the model), the main hand is filled before it is left empty, by the smaller (key, tier), and then the off hand the same way; the other items go by the earlier rule. Only arrangements worth exactly as much are candidates, and only those that come before the solver's own in that order are valued, so an arrangement that is already first costs nothing. What is checked after the rule has run is the items (copies counted), always, and the exact value, whenever arrangements were compared; a `PlacementError` says that they differ. The tie stage cannot change: its ranks belong to the items alone (`tie_ranks`), not to the places they stand in. Cost, measured on 300 scenarios drawn uniformly from the precompute grid (tiers 0, 4 and 10, wiki and strict proxy, own and group buffs): 0.77 ms per canonical solve on average for the two placements of a canonical solve (median 0.70 ms, 95th percentile 2.6 ms, largest 32 ms), against 0.74 s for the solve itself, about 0.1 percent, or 0.17 CPU hours over the 770,000 canonical solves of the precompute; arrangements were valued in 22 of the 600 placements, and a hand moved in 6 of them (3 of the 300 solves). On the fixtures, made again on the same data with and without the rule, 21 of the 571 canonical solves (16 of the 533 and 5 of the 38 solves of the best 2 and 3 sets) changed arrangement, and none changed its items, its value, its buffs or its tie stage. highs-js now ends in Python's kit place for place in every one of them, so the parity test no longer allows a tie of the same total rank, and a default view that is solved in the browser can be held to its precomputed shard kit place for place. Two different kits of the same total rank (different items) are still left to the solver, as the 2026-10-14 entry says; no solve of the fixtures differs between the engines.
- 2026-10-16 The phone gate: the farm path of the Upgrade tab streams, and the reweight sliders stay free. `scripts/phone-bench.mjs` measured the browser engine in Chromium 153 under the CPU throttle of DevTools at rate 6, on a desktop (Ryzen 9 3900X), over 40 matrix cases and 10 Upgrade cases of the full fixture set (data-2026-10-14, seed 20261014), and every answer was the value Python recorded. The throttle slows the page's main thread only (a dedicated worker ran at full speed), so the engine ran there. It slowed plain JavaScript 6.7 times and the solver 9.9 times, so this proxy is harsher than a phone 6 times slower than the desktop. Under it, one farm step (every Best-1 solve a visitor waits for, 69 of them) has a median of 0.45 s and a 95th percentile of 6.4 s (largest 9.5 s). That is within the 8 s that streaming needs, so step 1 starts by itself and later steps are added as they finish, up to 8 steps or a soft budget of 60 s, with Stop. The whole 8-step path has a median of 2.9 s and a 95th percentile of 39 s (largest 56 s), and Equip now a median of 0.08 s and a 95th percentile of 2.0 s. A full-pool solve (pruning, model and solve, placement only) has a median of 1.8 s and a 95th percentile of 6.7 s (largest 11.0 s). That is within the 10 s above which the sliders would snap to precomputed positions, so custom weights stay on the sliders. The pruning was timed on its recorded input in 5 of the 40 cases and on the already-pruned pool in the others, which understates it; it took under 0.2 s throughout. The cold start is 0.11 s from the WebAssembly in memory to a ready solver, but the first solve of a fresh page then takes about 4 s more, because the browser compiles the solver's functions when they are first called. So when the engine is wired into the page, its worker runs one small warm-up solve as soon as the solver has started, before the visitor's first request. The phone targets are now these. Under this proxy, the gate: at the 95th percentile, one farm step takes at most 8 s and a full-pool solve at most 10 s. The earlier targets (a median under 1 s per solve and a 95th percentile under 5 s) stay the aim for a mid-range phone, about half as slow as the proxy. The whole path has a soft budget of 60 s.
- 2026-10-16 A best set may copy an item it upgrades. The review of the canonical hands found that the best k additions made at once could be worth less than the first k steps of the farm path (upgrade-012 of the fixtures: the best 2 set was 13819.16 and the best 3 14225.15, against 14539.42 for step 2). The path upgrades an owned item (the same key at a lower tier) into the candidate, and a later step adds another copy of the candidate, which is a `copy` by then because the upgraded item is owned. The set model could not say that: the builder tied every wear of an upgrade's target to a copy of its source (`own.<source>` covered the target's columns), so wearing the target twice took two owned copies of the source. By the semantics of `upgrade.py` the k steps of the path are k additions, so every path of k steps must be a feasible set of k additions, and the best set of k is never worth less than step k. The rule: only the first copy of an upgrade's target is made from the owned source, one copy of it; every further copy of the target is a copy of the target, one addition each, as in the path (once the target is owned it is a copy candidate, never an upgrade again). Upgrading two copies of the source costs as many additions as one upgrade and one copy and leaves fewer of the source, so the model never needs it. In the builder (`_ownership_rows` in `model.py`, and the same in `model.js`), a target that can be worn more than once (not Lore, and the sum of the upper bounds of its columns, no more than `max_new` when that is set, above 1) has a 0/1 integer column `made[<target>]` and the row `made.<target>`: the sum of its columns less that sum times `made` is at most 0, so a worn target has `made` 1; `own.<source>` counts `made` in place of the target's columns, and every wear of the target is still one of the `max_new`. A target that can be worn once at most keeps the rows it had, so every program with one new wear, which is every program of the farm path, is the program it was, row for row and column for column. `acquired` of a best set lists each upgrade followed by the copies of its target the kit wears, then the new items, then the copies of owned items above their counts, each group by ident (it was the upgrades, the new items and the extra copies by ident, with an upgrade listed once for each wear). The owned-aware pruning reads no bound on the wears of a target from the count of its source (a source with enough copies has places for its own wears and the copy made for each worn target, so its row still never binds), and an item that replaces a target wear for wear costs no more acquisitions than the target did, so it is unchanged and its text only says that the first wear is the one that costs a copy of the source. On the fixtures made again from the same seed and data (the 19 Upgrade sessions of the full set, of which the committed sample holds 6): Equip now, the steps, why each path stopped and Every slot are the same in every session, and so is every program of Equip now and of the steps; 3 of the 38 best-set entries changed (upgrade-002 k=3, upgrade-012 k=2 and k=3), each to a higher value, and 30 of the 38 best programs have a different model (the `made` columns and rows added, 288 of each over the 30 programs and 144 distinct, and the 288 `own.` rows of their sources changed to count them). Every best set is now at least as good as the path after as many steps (33 comparisons in the full set, 7 in the sample). A limit that stays: each candidate is classified against the owned set the session starts with, and the tier modes offer one candidate per key, so a set does not reach the orders in which a path makes a later candidate of the same key new because an earlier one used the last copy of the lower tier it would have upgraded; with two candidates of one key a set could be worth less than the path.
- 2026-10-16 The engine's data travels as the shards do: the bundle of a trio and the item table are result files of the precompute, never committed. A run writes `<trio>/engine-l<level>.json` (`export.browser.bundle`) and `<results>/items.json` (`export.browser.items`, one file for the folder) from the catalog document it was given and the private seen flags it was given, so that their evidence levels are the ones the shards were solved with: a seen item is strict in the item table, and in the pools the bundle's weapon setups of each level are read from, unless a patch or research removal hides it. Nothing in either file says that a flag was merged (no reason code, no hash). The files are made at the start of the run, checked against what the run read for itself (the usable items of the trio and their levels; the bundle's setups against the grid's), and written with the shards (the item table first; an identical one is left alone, another is replaced whole and the run says so). A file that cannot be made (a pool whose weapon setups depend on the race, an item the tier rules cannot read) is a violation of the run, as an oversized shard is, not a stop. The module that holds the rule of the levels (`precompute/grid.py`) is hashed into the fingerprint of the chunks and was not touched. The site build reads both through `site/data.py` (the format number, the trio, the level, the release and the rules version of the shards, the exporter's own reader on the bundle, every item a shard shows against its record), publishes them byte for byte under `assets/engine/data/` as `items-<HASH>.json` and `<trio>-l<level>-<HASH>.json` (the first 16 digits of the SHA-256, in capitals) and lists them in `engine-manifest.json`, which is version 2: `"items"` and, for each trio, `"bundles"` beside `"levels"`, a bundle for each level and for no other, each with its SHA-256 for the worker to check before it parses a byte. (The bundles were moved out of the main manifest into a manifest for each trio; see the entry on the engine's manifests below.) Budgets, enforced by the build and named in its errors: a bundle 64 KB raw, the item table 4 MB raw and 512 KB gzip (the real item table of PAL/MNK/BER's catalog is some 1.9 MB and 182 KB gzip, a bundle 21.6 KB). The data files carry no `license` or `attribution` of their own, since the bundle's and the table's formats are exact and the footer of every page carries the one attribution; the item names in the table are the wiki's, and a page that shows them says so.
- 2026-10-16 Equip now is the first scenario mode the worker answers. In the browser, `static/engine/scenario.js` answers a scenario request of the mode `equip-now` (the best kit from the owned items alone; the request, `{v, kind, trio, level, mode, scenario: {race, role, buffs, evidence, setup, deity?}, owned: [[ident, count], ...], budgetMs}`, and its answer are checked in both directions by `protocol.js`): the worker fetches the item table and the bundle of the trio on the first request for it, by the manifest's paths alone, checks the type and size as the body is read and the SHA-256 before it parses a byte, keeps the item table and the last trio's bundle, and keeps the session for the next request of the same scenario. `SCENARIOS_READY` of `client.js` became `SCENARIO_MODES_READY`, a set of modes: `equip-now` is in it and the others still fail at once with not-implemented, before the question. The test of the two builders over the same scenarios that the entry of 2026-10-14 asked for is the Equip now answers of `fixtures/site/equip-now.json` (three scenarios, made by Python's `upgrade.equip_now` with the tie stage; the browser has none, and the owned sets are chosen so that the best kit is the same without it), which the worker's handler and a real browser are held to. An `ExactnessError` or a `PlacementError` of the solver is `internal`, not `bad-request`: it is a fault of the engine, not of the input. The warm-up solve that the phone-gate entry above asks for when the engine is wired into a page is not part of this step.
- 2026-10-16 The engine's manifests: the main manifest lists no bundles, and each trio has a manifest of its own. The first layout of `engine-manifest.json` (version 2) listed the bundles of every trio with their path, sizes and SHA-256, some 218 bytes a trio: for the planned 560 trios that is 122 KB at one level and 245 KB at two, and the page and the worker refuse a manifest over 64 KB (`LIMITS.maxManifestBytes` of `protocol.js`), so a full build passed and every optimizer was dead. Now `engine-manifest.json` holds the optimizer's files, the item table, the release of the data (`data_release`, `rules_version`) and, for each trio, only its levels (`"trios": {"pal-mnk-ber": [50]}`): 560 invented trios at two levels are 12.9 KB, some 22 bytes a trio. The bundles of a trio are in `engine-trios/<trio>.json` (`{version, data_release, rules_version, trio, bundles: {"<level>": {path, bytes, gzip, sha256}}}`, 457 bytes for such a trio at two levels), at a fixed address outside `/assets/`: like the main manifest it keeps the host's default caching and is asked for again on every visit, so `site/_headers` has no rule for it (the only caching rule matches `/assets/*`, and a test says that no rule of caching matches either manifest). The worker makes the address only from the name of a trio that the main manifest lists, never from the text of a request; it reads the file with a size limit of its own (8 KB, `LIMITS.maxTrioManifestBytes`), as strictly as the main one (only the fields it lists, level names without a zero in front, in the key and in the file name of the bundle, so that "50" and "050" cannot be one level twice; a bundle for each level the main manifest lists for the trio and for no other; a bundle whose file name carries its trio, its level and its digest), and refuses it if its `data_release` or `rules_version` is not the main manifest's. The build checks the main manifest and every trio manifest against the browser's limits (a build over a limit is a `BudgetError` naming the file) and a test holds the numbers of `build.py` to those of `protocol.js` and `manifest.js`; a test renders the manifests of 560 invented trios at two levels, and has the browser's reader read them. The version of the main manifest stays 2: its first layout never left the branch it was made on. The question that asks for consent names the download of the request: for a scenario it counts the item table and the bundle of the trio as well as the worker and the WebAssembly (the page reads the manifest of the trio before it asks: a few hundred bytes, no data); a question asked before any request counts the item table and no bundle, and a model request counts neither. The figure is for the first request: a later request for another trio fetches that trio's bundle, a few kilobytes, which it does not count. After a release the files that a live worker names are gone and the manifest of a trio is the new release's: when the data of a scenario fails to load, or the manifest of its trio is of another release than the worker's manifest, the page reads `engine-manifest.json` again, and if that is another text than the one it began with it shows the notice that the page has to be reloaded (`updated`) instead of an error.
- 2026-10-16 The time of a request to the engine, and its faults: the budget of a request is the time its solve may take, counted from the moment its data is in, and the page's watchdog gives a request a new window (its budget and five seconds) at every sign of life. The first Equip now downloads its data (the manifest of the trio, the item table, the bundle) inside the request. Counted from the arrival of the request, the budget of a request on a slow link was gone before the solver was called, and the watchdog, armed once for the budget and five seconds, stopped a worker that was only slow (512 bytes every 300 ms with a budget of a second: stopped at 6.0 s, twice, the item table fetched again each time). Now every report of progress of a request re-arms its watchdog (the post of the request is the first sign of life); the worker reports every second while it loads the data, whether bytes arrive or not (`LOADING_BEAT_MS`), so a worker that is waiting for a download is not taken for a hang; a download that has stopped is ended by its own stall rule (20 s with no byte, counted from the request, from the answer and from each piece: the answer re-arms it too) and not by the watchdog; and `elapsedMs` of the answer still counts the whole request. Faults are told by what they are. A `SolverFault` (a subclass of `SolveError`: HiGHS refuses a model the builder made, the run ends in a status the solve does not accept, the solution has an entry that is not a number or a column that is not whole) is `solver`, and the session that met it is closed. A bundle whose rules the engine cannot use is refused when it is loaded (its tier and weapon rules are compiled then, not at the first owned item) as `engine-load` and is not kept; a bundle that no session can be made of, and an item that the table writes so that it cannot be worked out, are `engine-load` with that data dropped, so that the next request fetches it again. What the visitor chose (a name the bundle does not have, an item the table does not have) stays `bad-request`.
- 2026-10-16 The build reads the engine's data as the browser does, and tells files of other inputs apart. `site/data.py` took item tables and bundles that the worker's readers refuse (17 deities or races in a record, an item type of 41 characters, a name with U+2028, 41 reason codes, a stat named `FOO` that passes both readers and makes `atTier` throw, a bundle nested too deep or with too many exact numbers). Now it holds both files to the limits of the readers (`RECORD_LIMITS` and `BUNDLE_LIMITS` in `data.py` against `RECORD_LIMITS` of `items.js` and `BUNDLE_LIMITS` of `profile.js`, and its vocabularies of stats and resists against the engine's, one test for the numbers), and after it has published the data the build runs the engine's own modules on it in Node (`site/check_engine_data.mjs`): `readItemTable` and then `atTier` at every tier from 0 to 10 for every record, and for every bundle `readBundle`, the compilation of its tier and weapon rules and `assembleProfile` for every race, role, buff set and evidence level it lists; a refusal is a `BuildError` that names the file and the record, and the bundles must all have the same tier and weapon rules, since the table is worked out under one set. The keys of items are canonical (a page id has no zero in front, so `w07` is not another way of writing `w7`, in the table, in `data.py` and in the idents of a request), a page names one item, the stats and resists of a record are named as the model names them, and the `source` of an item has the shape the exporter writes it in (the places it drops, is sold and is foraged in and its quests, at most so many, none twice, texts of at most 200 characters with no control or separator character; a count of recipes; and in `more` how many places did not fit in a list that is full). The item table was shared by every trio of a results folder and the build noticed another catalog or another set of seen flags only when a shard showed a changed item. Now the precompute puts a fingerprint of its inputs, `inputs`, in `items.json` and in the bundle of each trio (an optional field of both formats, after `rules_version`): the SHA-256 of the catalog file it read and of the key and evidence level of every item of the table, the flags merged. It is made of what the published table says and not of the flags, which is not what was first asked (a digest of the catalog and of the list of flags): a digest of the list of flags could be tried against guesses about flags that change no level (a seen item that a patch removed, say), and this one is a function of the published levels and of nothing else; a flag that changes no level changes no byte of either file. The build refuses a folder whose trios and `items.json` do not share one, naming each trio to run again, and the worker refuses a bundle and a table whose fingerprints differ. A run replaces the files of its trio first (all or nothing, the defaults last) and `items.json` after them, so that a write that fails leaves the table as it was, and a table that cannot be made is removed, as a bundle that cannot be made is: no stale file stands beside new ones. The build also holds the weapon setups of the bundle to the levels of the table: the shield setup of a level needs a shield in the pool of that level, and a pool with a shield that every race can wear needs the setup (a shield that only some races wear may or may not make it, and the table cannot say which).

The 20 are now invented items, and an invented item whose name was a real one has another. Their keys are from a range no item page uses (`w80113` to `w80988`, and `w8091`), chosen so that the order of the keys as text and as numbers is the one the fixture depends on: the rows of a shard are sorted by key as text and the kits name rows by index, the item table is sorted by key as a number, and the invented `w90xxx` items still come after. A stand-in is not to be traced to the item it stands for, by its key, its name (no paraphrase), the places of its source (the kind of source is kept) or a number: no stat, resist, weight or weapon damage or delay of a stand-in repeats the value of that item. Changing a key, a name or a place of a stand-in changes no kit and no answer of the fixture. `test_site_fixture_items.py` reads `derived/catalog.json` and fails when the key of an item of the wiki (as a token, or as the page of an item record) or its name is in anything under `fixtures/site`, in `tests-e2e` or in `engine_fixture.py`: a name of three words or more as a run of words anywhere, a name of one or two words as the whole of a string (a one-word name spelt as the wiki spells it, because the wiki has items named File, Key and Note). It never reads the observations. If the wiki grows to a page whose id the fixture uses, the test names the key and the fixture gets another.
- 2026-10-16 The scenario modes are four, each with an allowlist of its own, and a farm path can be stopped between its steps. A scenario request is `equip-now`, `farm-path` (a tier mode and 1 to 8 steps), `best-sets` (a tier mode and k of 2 or 3) or `every-slot` (a tier mode, a kit of 22 places, the chosen buffs, a limit of 1 to 10); any other mode, the old generic shape and a list of candidates are bad requests. The path is a stepper in `path.js` (`createFarmPath`: `start`, `next`, `stop`, `result`) and `farmPath` is a loop over it, so that its results and the parity tests are untouched; its deadline is fixed when it is made, so the time a caller takes between two steps counts. The worker streams `step` messages (index 0 is Equip now), yields to the event loop between two steps (a macrotask, after the step is sent) and asks whether a stop has come. A `stop` is `{type, id}`, handled the moment it arrives and not behind the queue of requests, it ends the farm path of that id before the next step the worker begins after it has seen the stop, with the result `stopped: "stopped"` (or the reason the path had ended for), it does nothing for any other id or any other mode, and it gets no reply. The worker looks between two steps, after a yield of 0 ms; in a browser a stop that the page sent from the callback of a step message can be delivered after that yield, so the path can end one step later than where the page asked, and a test that delivers its messages as microtasks sees it end exactly there (the test of the real worker in a browser allows for it). It is graceful: the step being worked out is a solve that cannot be interrupted from the side, so it finishes (within what is left of the budget), is sent, and the steps made, the session and the worker are kept. `stop()` of the client applies to every farm path it has not finished, the one in flight and each one that waits its turn behind a model request or another path (a waiting one is marked, and its stop is posted right after its request, so that it ends after its Equip now); it never touches a request that is not a farm path. `cancel()` is the way out of a request that must end at once: it terminates the worker. The page does not trust the path it is sent: `createPathWatch` holds the steps to their order, their sums (a gain is exactly the value less the value before it) and what the kit may wear (what was owned and what the steps acquired), and a worker that sends a step that is not right is dropped. The results of the other modes are held to what the request named: what is acquired (a step, an addition of a best set) is at the tier of the tier mode (ready4: +4; fixed N: +N; arrives D: +D or +0, as the page cannot tell a drop from another item), a best set's kit wears each addition it lists, the rows of Every slot name the items of the kit the request sent, and a swap is an owned item or one at the tier of the mode. Every step is a sign of life for the watchdog, as progress is, and every report of a scenario request carries the stage it is in. The report of a step is sent from the stepper's `onBegin` (the step has passed the checks of the stop, the limit and the budget, and its plan is not made yet), so that none names a step that does not begin.
- 2026-10-16 The worker makes the candidates; the request names a tier mode. The candidates of a farm path, a best set and Every slot are the records the precompute offers, that the profile can use: `select_items` keeps a record whose evidence level is strict or wiki and that is not marked `not-for-bis` (a temporary item, or one a quest takes on hand-in: the table writes the code in `evidence.codes`), `Context.candidates` offers those whose level is in the scenario's set ("strict": strict; "wiki": strict and wiki, deity-locked items included), and the profile must be able to use the record (`usableBy` of `path.js`, the model builder's own rule and upgrade.py's usability check: a class of the trio, a race and a deity it admits); a hidden record (`owned_only`) is never one. Each is read at `tierForMode` through `atTier` with the bundle's rules. Every slot offers the visitor's owned items as well, as the Python reference does (`_every_slot` in `fixture_cases.py`: its pool is the candidates and the owned items, one entry for an ident): an owned item at the tier it is owned at is a swap whatever the tier mode, a hidden record the visitor owns included, and `everySlot` of `path.js` leaves out what the profile cannot use. A key has one candidate by construction, which closes the 4d follow-up: the protocol carries no candidates, so no request can send two of one key. The last list is kept, with the SHA-256 of the table and of the bundle, the race, the deity, the evidence level and the tier mode. On the site fixture an Ogre has 43 strict candidates and 44 wiki ones (46 for a follower of the Tribunal), made in under 3 ms; on a table of the real size (6,756 records) the wiki set has 1,678 and takes about 10 ms. The cost of a step or of an addition is `costToReach` from the tier of the copy an upgrade is made from, else from the tier the item arrives at (with the D of the tier mode, 4 otherwise); it is null when the mode scores the item below the tier it arrives at, as in the reference.
- 2026-10-16 The worker warms up the solver before it says it is ready. The phone gate found about four seconds of lazy compilation of the solver's code on the first real solve under a six-fold throttle (0.4 s without one), paid after the visitor had asked. Right after HiGHS starts, `loadHighsEngine` now solves one small built-in program (`warmUpModel`: forty yes/no columns, five knapsack rows from a generator with a fixed seed, an optimum of 1527, some thirty nodes of branching) with the options of every real solve, so that the start pays under the loading panel, inside the minute it already has. Unthrottled, in Node, it takes about 265 ms and the first Equip now of the site fixture takes about 150 ms after it instead of about 290 ms. The answer is known, so a warm-up that throws, does not finish or finds another optimum is a start that failed (engine-load, no `ready`): a solver that does not do what it should is not used.
- 2026-10-16 The input of the Upgrade section. The trio page ends with the section (`templates/upgrade.html.j2`, 2.3 KB of HTML: the real PAL/MNK/BER page is 57.6 KB of the 60 KB a trio page may be): a drop zone that is the label of a file input, an item picker (an ARIA list-autocomplete combobox, a tier select and Add) and the list of the visitor's gear, switched off until the page code enables them. Its code (the parser, the matcher, the readers of the item table, `static/ui/gear-picker.js` and `upgrade-input.js`: 57 KB raw, 21 KB gzip) is a chunk of its own, which `trio.js` imports on the first focus or click on a control of the section or the first file dragged over it or dropped on it, so the page script grew by 2.2 KB raw (0.7 KB gzip) and nothing more is loaded before. For that the page bundle is built with esbuild's `--splitting`. The budget of a first load counts the page script and the stylesheet only, so the build refuses a page script that imports a chunk at once, or that holds a file of the section's code (a static import makes esbuild inline it with no import statement at all), and a build in which no other output holds those files (`check_page_script`, read from esbuild's metafile), and `total_js` (100 KB raw) counts the chunk (80 KB in all). The section fetches the item table as the worker does: the manifest (asked for again each time, within the 64 KB the worker reads) has its data release compared with the page's (`data-release` of main) at each attempt's first read; then the table by the path, size and SHA-256 the manifest lists (`engine/download.js`), checked before a byte is parsed, read by `readItemTable`, once for the page. A release that is not the page's shows the page's own "updated" notice, with Reload, and the section says the same in its own line near its controls, with a Reload of its own; the table is not asked for, the section's controls are off (the focus goes to the Reload button if it was on one of them), and a file dropped or chosen afterwards is answered with the same words through `#announce` and kept nowhere. A failure says what failed in that line, with Try again, and a retry reads the manifest again; the focus stays on the button through the retries (a code of the section that could not be fetched, or would not start, is told in the same line with one Reload button and not a Try again, "Couldn't load the Upgrade section. Reload the page to try again.", since a browser keeps a failed module fetch until the page is reloaded: Chromium does, a second `import()` of the same address after a failed fetch does not work even when the network is back (a browser probe showed it, and the browser test reloads and then has the section); the next use of a control still tries once more without a word, for a browser that does not keep the failure; there is no cache-busting). Try again stays for the item list, which is fetched again. The section has no live region of its own and adds no `role="status"`: the page has two, `#status` and `#announce`, and what the section shows it says once through `#announce` (except the notice of a newer release, which `#status` says). The file is read in the browser with the parser, and nothing is sent or kept; its name is never looked at; a refusal of the parser is shown in the parser's own words. Matching: the rows the parser calls bags are not matched (they would only be listed as not in the catalog); a row that fits more than one item, or fits one only by a name that is close (stage 4, offered), is left out of the list and offered in a banner (a plain element) with a Choose button for each candidate (at most 50, and a line says how many more there are), which shows its slot and its key stats, grouped by the ID the file gives (the same name and ID is one item); nothing is picked for the visitor; a name that fits none is listed as not in the catalog and not scored. The list holds what was added by name first and then the rows of the file in file order, one row for each item at each tier with its count and where the copies are (worn, bag, bank, shared bank, cursor, key ring); a new file replaces what the last file gave and keeps what was added by name and the choices made (kept by the ID and the name of the row, dropped by Clear); removing a row of a chosen item sends the item back to needing a choice only when every row of it is gone. The list never holds more than the protocol takes: a count past 99 is used as 99 and an item past the 1,000th is left out, which a note says and a screen reader is told. The picker counts quotation marks as one, as the matcher does (the game writes many names with a backtick, the wiki with an apostrophe, a phone turns a typed one into U+2019), and after a pause in the typing (half a second) tells `#announce` how many items match (at once, when an arrow key asks for a list and nothing fits); an input method editor that composes keeps its Enter, arrows, Escape and Tab. Every drag of a file over the page is cancelled from the page's start and for good, by a guard on the window (the drop effect is none outside the section, the section's own over it): the browser would open a file dropped in place of the page, the gear entered would be lost, and the name of the file, which the game gives after the character and the server, would be in the address, so no failure of the section's code (a start that throws, handlers that are gone) may leave a file to the browser. A drag of text or a link is left alone. The page's hold on a file given before the section's code runs, and the chunk's hold before its gear input exists, are let go only once the next stage has started, so that a start that fails loses no handler and no file. The interface for the answers: `createGearInput(root, {items, trio, doc, win})` returns `{owned, toOwnedPairs, onChange, clear, readFile, takeFiles}` (`toOwnedPairs()` is `[[key@tier, count]]`), and `initUpgrade`, which `trio.js` runs and keeps as `app.upgrade`, returns `{gear, ready(), retry(), readFile}`.
- 2026-10-16 The answer of the Upgrade section. Under the list of the gear `static/ui/upgrade-answer.js` (in the same chunk: 11.2 KB more raw, 4.2 KB more gzip; the page script grew by 459 B, the page's HTML by 49 B: the empty container `#upgrade-results` and `data-level`, the level the page is for) draws Equip now, the Next step and Then (the line with Stop and the progress is above them, where no row pushes it down, and Stop takes only its first press: it is marked aria-disabled and keeps the focus; neither Stop nor the working line is there while the client's question waits for the visitor's answer, when nothing is worked out, and they come with the client's first report or step after a yes, or at once for a visitor who agreed before) from a farm path that the page's one engine client works out in the visitor's browser. The client's code is imported at the first request from the address that `engine-manifest.json` names (`client.path`, made absolute by the same same-origin check as the item table's), and `getEngineClient({window, document, mount})` is given the section's results area for its own question: the consent is the client's, the section adds none, and a "no" is answered with a line and a "Load the optimizer" button that calls the client's `askConsent`. A request is sent after a pause of 600 ms in which neither the list of the gear (`onChange`) nor the view the page shows (race, role, setup, buffs, evidence level: `trio.js` tells the section from `paint`, so a view that could not be loaded changes nothing) has changed: the path in flight is ended (`client.stop()`), then one request `{v, kind: "scenario", trio, level, mode: "farm-path", scenario, owned: toOwnedPairs(), tierMode: {mode: "ready4"}, steps: 8, budgetMs: 60000}` is given to the client. The client works out every request it is given in its turn, a superseded one too (it loads and solves Equip now for it), so the page gives it a request only when the one before is over and only if no newer one was made meanwhile: of the requests made while a long step ends, the last waits and the others never begin. A request that is the same as the one sent last (a view that came back, an item added and taken out within the pause) is not sent, and stops nothing, unless that one failed. Every request has a number; the steps, the progress and the result of one that a newer request superseded are never drawn, and the old answer is cleared when the newer one is sent. An empty list sends nothing, and ends the path at once and clears the results, as Clear does; a request that still waits for the visitor's answer to the question (consent unknown: no worker, nothing downloaded) is withdrawn with `client.cancel()` and not stopped, so that a yes loads nothing for an answer that is thrown away, while a request that is on a worker is only stopped (the worker is kept). The words of the page (`tierLabel`, `reachText`, the figures, "Leave empty", the names of the places of the kit) are handed to the section by `trio.js` and not imported: the page script imports `format.js`, and a module that the page script and the section share is split off by the build as a chunk that the page loads with it (`check_page_script` refuses that). Equip now is the kit place by place, with the tier of each owned copy; a copy that the kit wears and the file does not say is worn gets "in your bags / in your bank / in your shared bank: move to worn" (the copies the file says are worn are the first the kit wears; a copy added by name has no place and no note), from the list as it was when the request was sent. Its label is "The best kit from the gear you have" only when the solver proved it; a solve that ran out of time says "best found, not proven optimal" and one that found nothing "not found in time", the protocol's words. A step says what it is ("Get X +4", "Get another X +4", "Upgrade your X +2 to +4"), its gain as the runner-ups show a difference ("Gain +1,234 kit value"; a gain above nothing that rounds to nothing is "+<1"), and, for the Next step, its source as the page words one (where it drops, "quest: ...", "vendor: ...", "crafted", "Foraged in ..."; the item table has no NPC) and what reaching the tier costs: a new item or a copy says what the page's Arrives-at column says (`reachText` of the tier it arrives at, so "16 copies to reach +4", counting the copy that arrives), an upgrade says the copies the worker costed ("12 copies to reach +4"). The spare-copy hint is "or upgrade your spare +2 copy (cheaper)" or "(the same cost)", for a `copy` step, when the visitor owns a lower copy of the item that the kit after the step does not wear (the copy that the step displaces is what it is for; the copies owned are the list with each step's acquisitions and consumed sources counted) and `costToReach` from its tier costs no more copies than another one, which is the step's `copiesAtArrival` (the merging) plus the copy itself, as the line's own "16 copies to reach +4" counts it (15 for a spare at +0 of an item that arrives at +0: cheaper; for a drop, which arrives at +4 and costs one drop, a spare at +2 costs one drop too: the same cost); the nearest lower copy is named, and each spare is offered once. One announcement is made when Equip now and the Next step are there ("Equip now is ready: 14 of 22 places filled. Next step: Get X +4, gain +435."), one when the path ends ("5 steps."), and one for a fault, joined with Equip now's when that was not said yet; nothing is said for each step, and the section has no live region (the client's own panel, while it is up, has its own). Focus does not move while the steps stream; when the control that has it goes, it goes to the control that takes its place (Try again to Stop and back) or to the answer. A fault is a line with what happened and a button: Try again (the optimizer's own words for the reason, except for a worker dropped for a message that did not fit the protocol, which has a fixed sentence), Reload when the site was released again (with the page's own notice, as the input of the section does) or when the code of the client could not be imported, Load the optimizer when the visitor said no (or cancelled the loading before the optimizer was agreed to; a request cancelled after it was, from the panel, is a request that was cancelled, with Try again); the steps already shown stay, with "The rest could not be computed."
- 2026-10-16 One runner-up file for each role, and the tiers of a run as its scope. The runner-ups were one file for each role and tier (`runnerups/<role>-<tier>.json`): 5,364 files for the 560 trios at +0, +4 and +10 (and the milestone trio at every tier), and 19,580 if all eleven tiers of every trio were precomputed, against the 20,000 files that Cloudflare Pages takes. They are now one file for each role, `runnerups/<role>.json`, with a view of every race at every tier of the run (`[tier, race, setup, places]`) and one table of items, and the index lists each with its `tiers`; the page fetches the file of the role the first time its runner-ups need one and reads the tier from it, so that a change of tier fetches nothing (the browser's file is version 2, `RUNNERUPS_VERSION`). A tier is a row and never a file: the 560 trios have 1,780 runner-up files, and the whole site 8,778 files with 2,500 item pages. `build.site_files` lists the files of a site exactly from its inputs (the trios with their roles and tier scopes, the item pages and the page code), a test holds that list to the files of a build of three trios, path for path, and the build refuses a site of more than 15,000 files (`LIMITS["files"]`). A runner-up file is held to the budget of a shard, 200 KB raw and 50 KB gzip, as the entry on the budget of a data file said but `check_budgets` did not check: the largest, the Balanced file of PAL/MNK/BER with all eleven tiers (on the wiki level, as a run without the seen flags makes it), is 157,243 bytes and 21,205 gzip. `--tiers` is the scope of a precompute run and no longer a narrowing: +0, +4 and +10 by default, every tier for pal-mnk-ber. The shards already recorded their `tiers`; the reader now holds their rows to the record and the file of each role to it (a tier outside the scope, a tier missing and a tier twice are refused), so that a run that has all of its scope is not partial, and a run that narrows races, roles, evidence levels or buff sets, or stops at `--limit`, still is. The tier switch of a page offers the tiers of its shards and no others, and a link that names another tier is read as a value that means nothing (the default view). The chunk files of a run are untouched, so an earlier run's work is reused.
- 2026-10-17 The scope of a trio is held where the site is published, and +4 is needed to make a run. A run of any tiers is a full one (the tiers are its scope), so `pc.run("pal-mnk-ber", out, tiers=(0, 4))` is not partial and loads, and nothing in the results says that the milestone trio is short of its eleven tiers or a trio of the grid of its +10. An indexable build now refuses a trio whose shards have other tiers than `trioforge.scope.default_tiers` of it (`build.check_scope`, and `build.check_scopes` for the trios of the results; a release check calls the same), with a line that names the trio, both lists of tiers and the way out; a preview takes any scope. The scope of a trio lives in `trioforge/scope.py`, which the precompute and the build both import, so the site generator does not import the solver. The command line has no option that names a scope: only a caller of `build.build` (a test of the fixture, which is a sample of the milestone trio at three tiers; the check of a release) can pass `scopes=`, so the workflow that deploys cannot be told to publish another scope. The precompute refuses tiers without +4, the tier of the default view, before it solves or writes anything: such a run wrote no `defaults.json`, took an old one away and still said it was a full grid.
- 2026-10-17 The tiers of a trio are one list, and the build asks for that list. The reader already compared the races, roles and tiers of each shard with the first shard's; the refusal now says which of them differ and the lists of both (`the grid (races, roles, tiers) differs from strict-own.json: its tiers are +0, +4, and strict-own.json's are +0, +4, +10`). `TrioResults.tiers` is the one list that the four shards and the runner-up file of each role all hold, and an error for a trio put together by hand from files of other tiers; `build.check_scopes` asks it and no longer reads the first shard's. `robust.json` records the tiers of its seals, which are some of the tiers of the run: the reader now refuses one that is not a tier of the run, one twice or out of order, and one that no role has labels for. The results files carry `version`, the writer's `shards.FORMAT` (1), and the reader accepts that number alone, for the runner-up files and their index as for the shards (the layout of the runner-up files changed, the format number did not: the earlier layout is refused by its file names and says to run the precompute again). The runner-up file of the browser is a different file with its own `v` (2), which the page code checks.
- 2026-10-17 Every slot counts the copies of the owned items. The swaps of a place were taken from a pool of the candidates and the owned items, one entry for an ident, with no count, in the Python reference (`every_slot` of `upgrade.py`, and the pool `_every_slot` of `export/fixture_cases.py` made for it) and in the browser engine (`everySlot` of `path.js`, and the pool of `scenario.js`) alike. So an item the visitor owns once, and the kit wears in one place, was offered as a swap for another place (one ring owned once and worn on Finger 1 was offered for Finger 2); and since Equip now is the best kit of the owned items, every owned-only swap of that kind that gained was such a phantom, and the phantoms filled the swaps shown (this amends the entry of 2026-10-16 on the candidates, where Every slot offered an owned item whatever its count). The rule, written in the docstrings of both engines: a swap to an ident the visitor owns, and that is not a candidate of the tier mode, is offered for a place only while the visitor's owned count of the ident is greater than the copies the kit wears in its OTHER places; a candidate (an item that can be got at the tier) has no count limit, as the swap means getting another copy; an ident that is both owned and a candidate counts as a candidate; Lore stays as it is (a Lore item worn in another place is not offered, whatever is owned). The functions take the candidates and the owned items apart (`owned=` of `every_slot`, `owned` in the request of `everySlot`; none by default, then every item given is a candidate, as before), and the assumed haste is still the session's, that of the kit, the owned items and the candidates together. The page's check of an answer (`readRow` of `protocol.js`) cannot know the candidates, only the tiers the mode gives: a swap to an owned item at a tier the mode does not give cannot be a candidate, so it is refused as a bad reply unless the owned count is above the copies the request's kit wears; an owned item at a tier the mode gives may be a candidate, and is not held to its count (an answer that is wrong only there is the worker's to get right, and the parity fixtures hold it). The fixtures, made again with the same seeds and data (`python -m trioforge.export.fixtures --out fixtures/parity --sample` and `--out build/fixtures --seed 20261014`): only `expected.every_slot` of the Upgrade sessions changed (and the sizes and hashes the manifest lists for them), and every other file is byte for byte the same. In the committed sample 3 of the 6 sessions changed, 10 of the 132 rows (12 swaps left a row and 10 entered one); in the full set 11 of the 19 sessions, 23 of the 418 rows (27 swaps left and 20 entered). No shard, default or runner-up of the precompute depends on it: no Python module of the precompute, of the site build or `export/browser.py` imports `trioforge.upgrade` (the runner-ups are `runnerup_chunk` of `grid.py`, swaps of a solved kit among the public candidates, with no owned set), so no real result needs to be made again.
- 2026-10-17 The expanders of the Upgrade section, and the budgets of the script. Under Then the answer has three expanders in `#upgrade-more`, made by the chunk (the template gains nothing; the block is out of sight until the first Equip now is drawn, and out of sight and closed again when the list is emptied): **Best 2 and 3 sets** ("sets, not a path"), **Every slot** ("Swap only this slot") and **Tier mode**. Each is closed until the visitor opens it, and nothing of its is worked out before. Their requests go through the answer's one client (`getClient` of `upgrade-answer.js`; there is no second import of `client.js`, which could not start a second client under the Trusted Types policy name) and its rule of one request at a time: an open expander asks for what it lacks once the path has ended, Equip now is there and the request before it is over (the page keeps one promise, `inFlight`, which the requests of the path and of the expanders all set); the expander that was opened first is worked out first (one closed and opened again counts as the last opened, and the order is kept when the answer is worked out again after a change), the sets of 2 and 3 are two requests and Every slot is one, and a change that waits for its pause begins none. They copy the scenario, the owned pairs and the tier mode of the path's request (budget 60 s, as the path's; `best-sets` with `k`; `every-slot` with the kit and the optional buffs of Equip now and `limit: 3`; protocol.js takes them as they are, which a test holds). A counter, the epoch, moves with every change of the gear, the view or the tier mode (at the change, not after its pause), with every request that is sent and with Clear: a result of an earlier epoch is never drawn, and what an expander shows is cleared when the request that follows the change is sent. When that request is the same as the last one (a change undone within the pause, or a file in which only the place of a copy changed) nothing is sent: the epoch goes back to that request's, so that what the expanders drew, and the request they have on the client, stand (an answer that came in the pause was not drawn, and is asked for again), and the words about where the copies are, Equip now's notes and the spare of Every slot, are drawn again from the new list, which the request does not name. An open expander is worked out again after the path, a closed one when it is next opened. A change or Clear ends the request an expander has on the client with the client's `cancel()` (`stop()` ends farm paths only), which stops its worker at once: the new path does not wait for a solve whose answer is thrown away, up to the minute it is given, and the worker starts again from the compiled module the client kept, with no download. The request is ended when the new path is sent, not at the change, so that a change undone within the pause costs the solve in flight nothing. **Best sets**: each set is a block with its additions in the words of the Next step (new, another copy or an upgrade, with its source and its cost to reach the tier) and the spare-copy hint of the path (cheaper or the same cost, each spare offered once; the copies that the set itself upgrades are taken out before its lines are written, whatever the order of its additions), then "Gain over Equip now: +N kit value", with the solver's label when its solve ran out of time ("best found, not proven optimal"); a set that was not found says "Not found in time.", one that adds nothing "No addition gains anything.", one with fewer additions than its size how many gain anything; a line says how a set differs from the path ("The best k additions made at once; the path adds one at a time, so its first k steps can differ.", k being the size of the set: the best 2 and 3 can use other items than the first steps do). **Every slot** says that it is an evaluation and not a solve, and what "you have it" and "get another copy" mean. The optimizer counts the copies of the owned items (the entry of 2026-10-17 on that): a swap to an owned item that is not a candidate of the tier mode is offered only while a copy is spare, and a candidate, an item that can be got at the tier, is offered whatever the visitor has, as a copy to get. So the page hides none of its swaps, and asks for 3 for a place. A first version, written while the optimizer kept no counts (one ring owned once and worn in Finger 1 was offered for Finger 2 as "you have it (in your bags)", and such swaps sorted first), asked for ten, dropped each swap of an owned item of which the kit wears every copy in other places, and showed the first three that were left; once the optimizer counted, that hid real swaps, often the best of a place (in the committed sample, upgrade-007: Waist +69 and the first Any slot +45 are offered the item the second Any slot wears, and each Wrist the item of the other; upgrade-002: the second Wrist +17), and could make a place say that none of its swaps could be made. It was taken out, and the tests read those cases from `fixtures/parity/upgrade/`, each item under an invented name. It is a table of the 22 places in the page's names with what the kit wears there and those swaps, each with its change in kit value as the page writes a difference ("+435", "−12", and "+<1" or "−<1" for a change that rounds to nothing) and, for an item on the list, "you have it" and where the spare is when a copy is spare (the copies the kit wears are the first ones, in the order Equip now's notes count them: worn, in a bag, in the bank, in the shared bank, on the cursor, on the key ring; an item added by name has no place, and nothing more is said), and "get another copy" when the kit wears every copy the visitor has; an item not on the list is said nothing of. **Tier mode** is Ready +4 (the default), As it arrives (a difficulty from +0 to +4, +4 by default, worded by `arrivalText`, which `trio.js` now hands to the section with the rest of the page's words) or Fixed (+0 to +10, +4 by default); choosing a tier chooses its mode; the summary always says the mode in force ("Tier mode: Fixed +6"); a change restarts the answer as any other does (stop, pause, one request; a mode chosen and then the one it was, within the pause, sends nothing), and the mode outlives Clear. **A fault in an expander** is said inside it in the words of a fault of the path (Try again; Reload when the site was released again, which tells the page too; "Load the optimizer" when the visitor said no), keeps what the expander had drawn, and leaves the answer of the path, its line and its failed mark alone. **Voice and focus**: one announcement when an expander's answer is drawn ("Best 2 and 3 sets are ready." after the set of 3, "Every slot is ready."; none if the visitor closed it meanwhile), or its fault, once, with its name; none for a change of the tier mode (the answer that follows speaks); no live region of its own; the focus stays on the summary that was opened, and goes to it when a button inside an expander goes. **The budgets of the script** are two now. The single one of 100 KB for all of it had no room for the section to grow (95,721 bytes, 93.5 KB, with the answer of the last task, and the expanders add 8.3 KB to the chunk). The first load (the page script and the boot script) keeps its numbers: 30 KB gzip and, raw, the 100 KB that the total had (`LIMITS["first_load_js_gzip"]`, `LIMITS["first_load_js"]`); it is 24,858 bytes raw and 8,827 gzip (24.3 KB and 8.6 KB), 14 and 8 bytes more than before, for the one word the page hands over. The code loaded on first use (every script directly under `assets/` that no page loads by itself: the chunk of the Upgrade section) has 100 KB raw and 40 KB gzip of its own (one budget for all of it, as it was then: the review of the shared kit made it one for each chunk and one for all of them, in its entry below), and the build refuses a build over either; the stylesheet that esbuild writes for a chunk (the real build writes none) counts in it, raw and gzipped, and the stylesheet a page loads stays in its first load. The build writes script of three kinds and no other: the first load's (the files the pages name), the code loaded on first use, and the optimizer's client and worker; script at the root of the site, in a folder of its own below `assets/`, or in the optimizer's folder under another name is refused, named, and not only reported. All the script but the optimizer's is reported, and has no limit of its own. Each of the four limits is tested at the limit and one byte over. Before and after, on the real results: the chunk 70,877 bytes raw and 26,106 gzip (69.2 KB and 25.5 KB), now 79,370 and 29,057 (77.5 KB and 28.4 KB: 77.5% of its 100 KB and 71% of its 40 KB), and 80,154 and 29,384 after the first review of it (78.3 KB and 28.7 KB: 78.3% and 72%); all the script 95,721 bytes, now 104,228 (101.8 KB), and 105,012 (102.6 KB) after the review, which is over the old limit of 102,400 bytes and would have refused the build; the trio page's HTML 59,041 bytes (57.7 KB of 60 KB) before and after, the template being untouched; the stylesheet 554 bytes more raw and 135 more gzip. Those sizes are of the main before the runner-up files per role and the counting of the copies; on the main with them, and the results of their layout, the chunk is 80,128 bytes raw and 29,370 gzip (78.2 KB and 28.7 KB), the first load is 25,331 bytes raw and 8,977 gzip (24.7 KB and 8.8 KB: the page script is 473 bytes larger with the page code of the runner-ups), all the script but the optimizer's is 105,459 bytes (103.0 KB), and the trio page's HTML is 58,772 bytes (57.4 KB of 60 KB).
- 2026-10-17 Share, shared kits and "Remember my gear". **The address.** `static/first.js` is the first import of the page script (a module is evaluated in the order it is imported), so `takeKitFromLocation` has moved the `#k=` payload into `history.state` and removed the fragment before the code of any other module of the page script runs; it hands the result (`{payload, source, cleared}`) to the page code. The function lives in `input/kitlocation.js` (1,151 bytes of the page script, with `first.js` 151) and the codec (`input/kitcode.js`: `decodeKit`, `encodeKit`, the tables; `decodeKit` has since moved to `input/kitdecode.js`, see the entry of the review below) is code of the Upgrade section: the two may not share a module, since esbuild would split a shared module off as a chunk that the page loads with it, which `check_page_script` refuses. So the address section moved out of `kitcode.js` with its tests (verbatim, to `kitlocation.test.mjs` and `kitlocation-fuzz.test.mjs`), and the build now also refuses a page script that lacks `first.js` or `input/kitlocation.js` (`PAGE_INPUTS`), and one that holds a file of the section (`SECTION_INPUTS` gained the codec, `share.js`, `shared-kit.js` and `remember.js`). Whether the fragment could be cleared is kept in one place, the `data-kit-fragment` attribute of the `<html>` element, for the analytics of a later phase: `cleared` (it was taken out of the address before any other script ran), `kept` (the browser refused to rewrite the address and it is still there), absent (no kit fragment was in the address; a reload has the payload from its history entry). The navigation entry of the page keeps the address it was loaded with whatever the page does, so an analytics build stays off for a page whose attribute is `cleared` or `kept` unless its own test shows that the beacon never sends `#k=`. The skip link no longer follows its anchor (a new history entry has no state, and a reload of it would lose the kit): the page scrolls to `#main` and moves the focus there; a click that opens the link elsewhere is left alone. **When the section's code loads.** A kit link, or remembered gear, starts it at once instead of at the first use; the check for gear is one guarded read of `localStorage` under `trioforge-gear` (the key is the page script's; the format is the section's), and a page with neither loads nothing more than before. If the code of the section cannot be loaded for a kit link, `#status` at the top says "Couldn't load the shared kit" with a Reload, besides the section's own line. **Share.** A "Share this kit" button in Equip now, once it shows a kit, with the words "This link lists the items in this kit.". The link is the page's own address (its origin and path: no query, no fragment of the page's: the link lists the items of the kit and not the view of whoever shares it), `#k=`, and the canonical text of the kit (`encodeKit`): the place, page id and tier of each item, at the race the answer was asked for; the owned list, counts, places, a value or a word are never in it. It is copied on the click and at no other time; when the browser has no clipboard or refuses, the link is in a read-only field, selected, labelled "Copy the link from here" (selected with `setSelectionRange`: Chromium takes the focus to the field on `select()`, and the button keeps it); an item whose key is not `w` and a page number is left out and counted ("N items of this kit cannot be shared"). **A shared kit.** "A kit shared with you" at the top of the answer area, before Wear this, with a line that says it is a shared kit. The link is decoded only once the item list is in (the section's loader), so "This kit link could not be read" is said only after the decode failed; the kit is drawn in the page's words (the places of the kit table, `tierLabel`, "Leave empty") with a placeholder, `Unknown item (w<page id>) +<tier>`, for each page id the list does not have, and "Excludes N unknown items" in the value line. A link of another trio than the page's or another level than the bundle's is said plainly and not valued. Otherwise the value is worked out in the page, under the view the page shows (the role, buffs and evidence level of its switches; the race and the level are the kit's, and so are the weapon setup and the haste, which this first version took from the page's view and the review below took back), with the engine's own evaluator (`assembleProfile`, `atTier`, `evaluate`), and again when a switch changes it: no worker, no download of the optimizer and no question, only the bundle of the trio by the paths and SHA-256 of the manifests (`loadKitBundle`, written like the worker's loader). An item that cannot be worn where the link puts it (the place, the trio's classes, the race, a deity, a second copy of a lore item) is left out of the value, says why in its row, and is counted in the line. The optional buffs were first chosen one at a time (each step adds the buff that adds most, within the set's limit and conflicts; 180 to 350 evaluations), which is not the best set (the review below replaced it with an exact search), and without optional buffs a group kit would be worth 20% less than it is. A kit shared from Equip now may show another number than its sender saw: Equip now scores weapons at the haste of the gear the sender owns (a haste item the sender has and does not wear counts there), a shared kit at the haste of what the kit itself wears. Its own Share link is made from the decoded kit (so it is canonical, and placeholders keep their page ids and tiers so that the next page can know the items) and is not offered for another trio or level. **Where the evaluator lives.** The evaluator, the profile reader and `atTier` are 41 KB of script (13 KB gzipped); in the section's chunk they would have taken it to 121 KB raw and 42.7 KB gzipped, over both limits of the code loaded on first use (100 KB and 40 KB). So `engine/kitvalue.js` (`valueOfKit`, `loadKitBundle`) was exported from `engine/client.js`, the optimizer's page-side file, which the manifest names and the page already imports by address; importing it starts nothing. The engine's two scripts, 122,157 bytes gzipped of the 150 KB they may have, held it. (That put the optimizer's code on every kit link, which `build.py` says is loaded on request and never on a first view; the review below made the evaluator a file of its own.) **Remember my gear on this device.** An unchecked box. Checked, the owned list is kept under `trioforge-gear` as `{v: 1, entries: [[page id, tier, count]]}` and written again at every change: no place, no name, no file name; at most 500 entries (past that the section says how many were left out), only for an item whose key is `w` and a page number (another key is counted and said), and an empty list is not kept. Unchecked, the copy is erased and the section says so. Every call to the storage is in a `try`: a device that is missing, full or blocked is said once ("Couldn't remember your gear ...", and again when a write works) and the section works as before. A page that finds some restores it through the checks any input has: the shape of each entry, the item list (an item the catalog no longer has is dropped and counted, "N remembered items are no longer in the catalog"), the protocol's limits (the list clamps a count to 99 and a list to 1,000 items, with its own notes) and the keys; the copy is written again without what was dropped, the box shows checked, and a line says how many were restored and that "Restored gear has no places, so Equip now gives no location notes for it", with a Forget (erase the copy, uncheck the box, clear the list, focus on the box). What another version stored, or what is not in the shape this version writes, is erased and said once. The box is made after the answer, so the answer sees the list fill and asks for its path as for any gear (with the optimizer's question, as ever). A restored item was first listed as "added by hand" (it says "remembered" since the review below): it has no place. **Budgets.** Before and after, on the fixture (the chunk does not depend on the data): the page script 24,956 bytes raw and 8,737 gzipped, now 27,482 and 9,549 (first load with the boot script 27,857 raw of 100 KB and 9,789 gzipped of 30 KB); the stylesheet 17,102 and 4,024, now 18,102 and 4,204; the chunk of the section 80,128 raw and 29,370 gzipped, now 98,885 and 36,130 (96.6% of 100 KB and 88.2% of 40 KB); the optimizer's client 59,118 and 18,768, now 107,719 and 34,575; the HTML of the trio page is untouched (the markup of the box, the button and the panel is made by the code of the section). A first version that fetched the bundle with the section's own code came to 101,783 bytes raw, 617 under the limit; the loader moved to the client file, which also keeps the readers of the manifest of a trio out of the chunk.
