- 2026-11-20 Part J: **(the design of the counter in this entry, a static tag that ran after the page script had cleared a kit link, was rejected by the review of Part J and is replaced by the Part M entry below: where this entry and that one differ, that one holds; what stands from here is the two settings, their checks, the header policy, the Search Console file and the comparison of a counting build with a plain one)** page counts and the Search Console file are switched on, as settings of the build (`site/build.py`, `templates/base.html.j2`, `templates/about.html.j2`, `scripts/preview.mjs`, `scripts/preview-plan.mjs`, `tests-e2e/analytics.spec.mjs`, `pipeline/tests/test_site_analytics.py`, `test_site_verification.py`). **The decision.** The user decided on 2026-11-20 to switch page counts on ("let's implement all 3": the counts, the words of the About page, the ownership file of Google Search Console). The site had been built for it since the 404 page entry of 2026-10-18: the frame has the place for a beacon (after the page script, in the block that the 404 page replaces whole), `first.js` takes a kit link out of the address first, and the 404 page keeps its own policy and no beacon. **Two settings, both off by default and neither in the repository.** `--analytics-token` (or `$TRIOFORGE_ANALYTICS_TOKEN` when the option is not given; the option wins; an empty variable is no setting) is the 32 hex characters that Cloudflare Web Analytics gives the site, and `--google-verification` (or `$TRIOFORGE_GOOGLE_VERIFICATION`) is the name of the ownership file, google followed by 16 hex digits and .html. A value that is anything else stops the build before anything is read, and the message does not repeat it. The token is public (it is in every page) but it and the file name are not committed: the tests use invented ones, and a test reads the documents for the shapes of the real ones. They are given where the release is built, on the command line or in the environment of that build; a preview never inherits them from the shell (`buildSettings` of `scripts/preview-plan.mjs`). **With neither, the build is what it was**: the plain build of the fixture is byte for byte the build before Part J (112 files compared), and a test holds a counting build to the plain one file by file (the tag, the policy line and the About words are the only differences). **With a token**, every page but the 404 page carries exactly one tag, `<script defer src="https://static.cloudflareinsights.com/beacon.min.js" data-cf-beacon='{"token": "..."}'></script>`, after the page script, and the header policy gains exactly `https://static.cloudflareinsights.com` in script-src and `https://cloudflareinsights.com` in connect-src (`ANALYTICS_CSP`; the committed `site/_headers` stays the policy with no host and is refused if it names one, and the build writes the counting one into its output). `check_beacons` holds the pages to that (once, after the page script, before the end of the body, mentioned nowhere else; none at all in a build with no token), `check_budgets` holds a page to three scripts where it had two, and the 404 page to two and no marker of a beacon, as before. **Keep the Cloudflare dashboard's own Web Analytics switch off**: it puts a beacon into every page by itself, the 404 page included (which its own policy refuses, and which `check_budgets` cannot see in the built files), and a second one into the pages that already have the tag. The build setting is the one way. **The Search Console file** is written at the root with the one line Google expects, `google-site-verification: <file name>`, no line end; it is no page, not in the sitemap, not linked, not counted among the pages, and one file more against the budget (`site_files` takes the name). Pages may answer a request for a file that ends in .html with a redirect to the address without the extension, which Google's check may not follow: if the first check fails, use the DNS record or the tag instead and drop the file. **What the beacon sees of the address on a trio page opened with a kit link** (a stand-in for the script, in `tests-e2e/analytics.spec.mjs`, that reads what a beacon reads of the address and sends it to the data host; the real script is never fetched, here or in any test): `location.href` and `document.URL` are the origin and the path and the query (the switches: they hold no personal information, and the About page says they may be counted), with **no fragment**, because the page script has run first (a module script and a deferred script run in document order) and `data-kit-fragment` is "cleared"; `document.referrer` is what the visitor's own referrer policy sent; no request to the two hosts carries the kit, a referrer, a cookie or a stored value; the only foreign requests are the script and the one POST. The test fails when the clearing of the fragment is taken out of the page script. **What is not gone, and cannot be from here.** (1) The navigation entry of the Performance API (`performance.getEntriesByType("navigation")[0].name`) keeps the address the document was loaded with, fragment included, for any script of the page; a test records that (it fails the day a browser cleans the entry). A beacon whose script sent that name would carry a kit, and what Cloudflare's script reads cannot be seen without fetching it, which was not done. (2) A browser that refuses `history.replaceState` leaves the fragment in `location` (`data-kit-fragment` is "kept"), and the static tag cannot be made conditional on it. [Corrected in Part M: the owner's rule is that kit codes are never counted, not that a kit is harmless; the check that this sentence named, a look in the Web Analytics dashboard for an address that holds `#k=`, cannot see the leak (a fragment is not a path, and the name of the navigation entry is shown nowhere in the dashboard), and is replaced by a read of the request body in DevTools.] **Trusted Types.** The page's policy is `require-trusted-types-for 'script'; trusted-types trioforge`. A static, parser-inserted `<script src>` is not governed by it, and neither is `navigator.sendBeacon`, `fetch` or an image request: only the DOM sinks (`innerHTML`, a script's `src` assigned from code, `eval`) and `trustedTypes.createPolicy` in the script's own code would be, and whether the real script uses them is not known without reading it. The stand-in runs with no violation; the weekly smoke check watches console errors, which is where a violation would show after a deploy. **The check after a deploy.** `scripts/smoke_live.mjs` compares the live headers with the file the site was built to: a counting build writes its own `_headers` into its output folder, so it is run with `--headers-file <output folder>/_headers` (a test shows that the check holds for the plain build with the committed file and for the counting build with its own). **Weight**, measured on the fixture with and without a token: every page gains 145 bytes raw and 73 to 80 gzipped (the trio page 55,294 to 55,439 raw, its first load 41,850 to 41,923 gzipped; the home page 8,456 to 8,601; the largest item page 9,757 to 9,902); no limit is near; the beacon script itself is Cloudflare's, deferred, and is in no budget of ours. The 404 page is the same bytes. **Accessibility** of the About page is unchanged (plain paragraphs; axe at level AA finds nothing in the counting build).
- 2026-11-20 Part K7 (docs only; the text was revised in Part M): a reading of Cloudflare's own beacon script, kept as background and **not relied on**. The architect read, and did not run, the public script of Cloudflare Web Analytics, version js "2026.10.1", on 2026-11-20; nobody has fetched it for the project's tests or builds, and none of the tests touches it (they use a stand-in). What the reading found: in its first send, the page address and the referrer both pass through a function that empties the hash, the search, the user name and the password of the URL before they are sent; it reads `document.location.href` at its start, and the reading does not say that an address sent later, after a change of the history, passes through the same function; it sends with `sendBeacon` or an XHR; it uses no cookie, no `localStorage` and no `sessionStorage`; it follows the changes of the history and the navigation entry for single-page sites unless its setting `spa` is false (which is the reading behind the `"spa": false` that the page script gives it); and the timing payload copies a fixed list of timing fields that does not include the name of the navigation entry, the one place that keeps the address a page was opened with. **Trusted Types: not seen in this reading.** The sinks that were looked for are `createPolicy`, `innerHTML`, `document.write`, `eval` and a script element made by code, and none was seen. Those are not all the sinks that `require-trusted-types-for 'script'` governs (not looked for: `Function()`, `setTimeout` or `setInterval` with a string, `insertAdjacentHTML`, `outerHTML`, `DOMParser`, `createContextualFragment`, `srcdoc`, and the addresses of a Worker or of `importScripts`), so whether the script runs under that policy is not known from this reading. **Why none of it is relied on.** It is a reading of one version of a minified script that Cloudflare can change at any time, without a word to the site: it is not a guarantee and it is not tested. The design of Part M therefore stands without it: a page opened from a kit link loads no counting script at all, so there is nothing for any version of the script to read; the setting `"spa": false` is given whatever the script does with it; and the check after the first deploy reads the request body itself (the Part M entry, and `docs/runbook-weekly.md`), instead of trusting this reading or the dashboard.
- 2026-11-20 Part M: page counts reworked after the review of Part J, which rejected it (`site/build.py`, `templates/base.html.j2`, `templates/notfound.html.j2`, `templates/about.html.j2`, `static/counter.js`, `static/tt-record.js`, `static/tt-policy.js`, `static/first.js`, `static/app.js`, `scripts/smoke_live.mjs`, `tests-js/counter.test.mjs`, `tests-e2e/analytics.spec.mjs`, `pipeline/tests/test_site_analytics.py`). **What the review found.** The order of Part J said to stop if the kit link could not be shown to be gone before the beacon read the address, and it could not: the navigation entry of the Performance API (`performance.getEntriesByType("navigation")[0].name`) keeps the address a document was loaded with, `#k=` included, whatever the page script does to `location`, and the last browser test of Part J recorded exactly that. The work went on, and the About page then promised that a count never held a kit. Three more holes followed from the static tag: a browser that refused `history.replaceState` ("kept") ran the beacon with the kit in the address; a page whose own script failed (a flaky network, an old browser) still ran the beacon, which comes from another host, with the kit in the address; and a kit link pasted into an open page raised `popstate` before `hashchange`, so a script that follows the history read it before it was cleared. The stand-in of the browser tests read only `location`, so none of this could fail. **The owner's rule** (`static/input/kitcode.js` has said it since the kit link was made) is that kit codes are never counted; the Part J sentence that called a kit link harmless explained the rule away, and is corrected above. **The design now fails closed.** No page of any build carries a script for the counter. A build with a token gives every page but the 404 page the token as inert data, `<meta name="trioforge-counter" content="TOKEN">` in the head (`counter_meta`; `check_counter` holds the pages to it: once, in the head, none on the 404 page, no mark of a beacon anywhere, nothing at all without a token), and the page script adds ONE script element for `https://static.cloudflareinsights.com/beacon.min.js` last (the end of `start()` in `app.js`, `counter.js`), with `data-cf-beacon` `{"token": ..., "spa": false}`, through the site's one Trusted Types policy, ONLY when the page was opened with no kit link (`first.js`'s take found no fragment and no kit in the history entry), `data-kit-fragment` is not set, no kit fragment is in the address now, the token is 32 lower-case hex (capitals are refused at build time), the page is not the 404 page, and the counter was not added before. A page opened from a kit link is simply not counted. **The rule for a reload**: a reload of the cleaned address finds the kit in `history.state`, so it is a kit page still and stays uncounted; a page opened again by its plain address, with no kit in its history entry, is counted. A page whose script does not load or throws before the end of `start()` is not counted either. **Trusted Types.** The page script and the optimizer's client are two bundles, and a policy name may be made once per page, so the record of the one policy `trioforge` moved to `static/tt-record.js` (no imports: the page script may not import a file that the lazy chunks share), kept under `Symbol.for("trioforge.tt-policy")` as closures only, and whichever bundle asks first makes the policy; it lets through the optimizer's hashed engine files and the ONE counter address, and the counter's way in gives that address and nothing else. The header directive is unchanged (`require-trusted-types-for 'script'; trusted-types trioforge`), and so is the header with a token: the same two hosts as Part J and nothing else. `first.js` now also takes the kit at `popstate`, the first of the pair that Chrome and Firefox fire for a pasted fragment. **One page script serves both builds** (it reads the data and does nothing without it), so asset hashes do not fork: a counting build is the plain build plus the one meta line in every page but the 404 page, the About words and the policy line, file by file (a committed test holds it). The plain build is therefore not byte for byte the build of Part I in one respect: its page script carries the counter code and the counter's address (+2,434 bytes raw, +818 gzipped on the fixture: the trio first load 41,738 to 42,556 gzipped against 61,440; the page script and the boot script together 20,826 gzipped against 30,720; the largest item page's first load 34,247 to 35,065). **What the design cannot do.** A script from another site runs in the page with full access to it, and a kit link pasted into an open counted page puts the kit in `history.state` (where a reload finds it) before the page loads again, so for those milliseconds a script that is already loaded could read it; the About page says that the script runs in the page. The visitor's IP address and browser details reach Cloudflare with the script and its report, and an address may carry parameters that other sites added (the About page says so). What Cloudflare stores, and what its script reads or sends, is Cloudflare's to say and is not tested: the stand-in records every address the page holds, and the real script is never fetched. **The About page** says only what the code guarantees and attributes the rest to Cloudflare (M2). **The check after the first deploy** is a read of the request body, not a look at the dashboard (the runbook has it). **Tests**: `tests-js/counter.test.mjs` (every rule above, the policy shared by two copies of the record), `tests-e2e/analytics.spec.mjs` (no kit link: one request for the script; opened with `#k=`: none, ever, also after a reload of the cleaned address; `replaceState` made to throw: none; the page script blocked: none; a kit link pasted into an open counted page: no address that holds the kit is ever seen by a stand-in that records `location.href`, `document.URL`, the navigation entry's name, `history.state` and the referrer at load and at every popstate and hashchange, and the test fails when the popstate listener is taken out; the 404 page: none), `pipeline/tests/test_site_analytics.py`.
- 2026-11-21 Part P: the second round on page counts, from the Opus review of Part M (accept with fixes). **P2, the history of the tab.** The review (finding 3) saw a way for a counted page to have a kit within reach: a kit page whose script did not run (or whose fragment could not be cleared) stays in the tab's session history with `#k=` in its entry's address, and a clean page that the visitor goes on to, in the same tab, could read it through `navigation.entries()`. What was found when the sequence was run in a browser (the review had it from the specification): the Navigation API gives the address of an entry of ANOTHER document as `null` when that entry's page was served with `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` or `origin`, and the site sends `no-referrer` (the committed `_headers`, which every build and the weekly smoke check hold): the clean page's `navigation.entries()` is `[null, <its own address>]`, so no script of it, the counter's included, can read the kit of the earlier page, and the page is counted (`tests-e2e/analytics.spec.mjs`: the stand-in reads the entries at load and sees no kit). The guard is there for the case where that is not so: `addCounter` does not add the counter when any entry that the page can see holds a kit (`navigation.entries()`, an entry's address that is not null, tested with the same `holdsAKit` as P4), inside a `try` that says "holds a kit" on any error; the browser test serves the kit page with `Referrer-Policy: unsafe-url`, which makes its address visible, and the clean page is then not counted (it was, before). **A stated limit**: where the Navigation API does not exist (Firefox and Safari at the time of writing) the history cannot be asked, and the behaviour is what it was without the guard; there, the protection is the one that the Referrer-Policy gives the API in the browsers that have it, and that no other API of those browsers lists the entries of a tab. **P6, the shared Trusted Types record (review finding 7, probed).** `globalThis[Symbol.for("trioforge.tt-policy")]` was a plain WeakMap: any script of a counted page could list a path of the shape of an optimizer file that the manifest never named, enable the counter's address on any page, and replace the record with `set` before the optimizer's client asked (the probe made the optimizer's worker address mint as the counter's URL). It is now a frozen object with one function, `get(trustedTypes)` (the map is in a closure; there is no `set`), on a property that is not writable, configurable or enumerable; each record is frozen; `allowCounter(address)` takes the counter's address and refuses anything else. The header comment of `static/tt-record.js` said "a script that finds the record can mint nothing the policy would not have minted for the site's own code": it now says what is guaranteed (the record cannot be replaced, redefined, deleted or changed) and what a stranger can still do (list an engine-shaped path, which mints a trusted address for a file that is not there; enable the counter's one address): there is no way to hide a record from a script of the page that two bundles must both find. The tests are `tests-js/counter.test.mjs`, which runs the reviewer's sequence. **P3, what the design cannot do (review finding 4; nothing in the page script can prevent it, since a running script cannot be unloaded).** A kit link pasted into a tab that is ALREADY counted is seen while the counter runs, until the page loads again. (a) The cleared variant: first.js takes the kit out of the address at popstate, so a listener that is added after first.js's (the counter's, as the real script would be) finds the address clean, and a test holds that for the reads at load, popstate and hashchange; but the Navigation API's `navigate` event (its `destination.url`) and `currententrychange` fire BEFORE popstate for a fragment navigation, so a listener there sees the kit before first.js can clear it, and `pagehide` fires as the old page is left: the claim of the comment in first.js that later listeners find a clean address holds only for popstate and hashchange, and says so now. (b) The kept variant: where `history.replaceState` is refused or throttled, first.js sets `data-kit-fragment` to "kept" and loads the page again, and until the old page is gone `location.href` holds `#k=` and a counter that reports at pagehide or visibilitychange could send it (the first version of this note named only `history.state`). These are LIMITS, not guarantees, and the About page says in one sentence that a kit link opened in a tab that is already counted can be seen by the script that is running there. The tests record them: the stand-in of `tests-e2e/analytics.spec.mjs` also reads at pagehide and at the Navigation API's navigate and currententrychange (annotations on the test, not assertions of a clean address), and a test of the kept variant asserts that the kit IS in the address while the counter runs (so that a change of the browser or of first.js shows up), that the page still loads again, and that the page that comes is not counted and does not ask for the script again; what they cannot show is what Cloudflare's script would do with it. **P1, the weekly check detects the dashboard's tag (review finding 1).** The one-click Web Analytics switch of the Cloudflare dashboard injects `<script defer src="https://static.cloudflareinsights.com/beacon.min.js" data-cf-beacon=...>` into the HTML it serves; the policy of a counting build allows both hosts, the tag is parser-inserted (Trusted Types does not govern it), the page script knows nothing of it, and the navigation entry of a page opened from a kit link keeps `#k=`: the Part J hole, with nothing to notice it (in a plain build the policy refuses the tag and the console error fails the weekly check). Only one sentence in CONTRIBUTING said to keep the switch off. `scripts/smoke_live.mjs` now fails, in BOTH modes, any page whose HTML as the server sent it holds "cloudflareinsights", "data-cf-beacon" or "beacon.min" (no page of any build carries one by design: the page script adds the counter); only the body of the response to the navigation is read, and only when its content type is HTML, which is how the page script's own bundle (which names the address) is told apart: it is another response, never read. With `--analytics` it also opens one trio page with an invented `#k=` fragment and fails on any request to either host of the counter. This is the harness-over-prompts rule: the instruction to keep the switch off is now enforced by the check that runs every week. The runbook and CONTRIBUTING say that the switch must stay off and that the weekly check now detects it. Tests: `tests-js/smoke-live.test.mjs` (both modes, the 404 page, the script that is not a page, the kit link page) and, in a real browser against the counting preview and a stand-in that injects the tag, `tests-e2e/smoke-live.spec.mjs`. **P5, the scan for the token and the file name (review finding 6, probed).** The test of Part M used `git grep -o`, which resumes after a match (so a second token on a line after one separator was missed: the invented one, allowed, ended the match), skips a UTF-16 file as binary (Windows PowerShell 5 writes UTF-16 for `>` and `Out-File`), knew no dashed spelling, and left `releases/` out whole. It is now a scan in Python over every file that `git ls-files` lists: UTF-8 and UTF-16 (a byte order mark, or a third of the bytes NUL in the even or odd places), the 32 hex digits of a token with lookarounds (every run on a line is seen), the dashed spelling (8-4-4-4-12), and the shape of Google's file name; a value is let through only if a test declares it as invented (`INVENTED_TOKENS`, `INVENTED_FILE_NAMES`, in one place, dashes left out), or is the field `snapshot_id` (the random id of a release's snapshot, on a line of its own) of a release pointer: nothing else under `releases/`, and the file-name shape nowhere. Not covered, and said: a token split over lines or written in base64, the history and the commit messages (the privacy scan's hook has those). The throwaway-repository test shows the old misses found (the old scan found none of four of its cases). **P7, the first-deploy check, `script.defer`, and the release's file count (review finding 8 and notes 9 and 11).** The runbook's first-deploy check now has what the review found missing: step (1), the report body of a page with no kit link, is said to show what the report holds and to be unable to show a kit; the kit link in a new tab says to open DevTools in the new tab before the link and preserve the log; a kit link pasted into a tab that is already counted, reading any later POST, the one at pagehide included; a kit page whose script is blocked (`app-*.js` in DevTools' request blocking), then a link, then `navigation.entries().map((entry) => entry.url)` in the clean page's console, which under the site's Referrer-Policy shows `null` for the earlier page (so the clean page may be counted, and its POST holds no kit; if the console shows the address of the kit page, there must be no request to either host). The review's expectation for this last step was "no request", which was what the specification suggested; run in a browser (the P2 entry), the entry's address is hidden, and the step says what to expect. `script.defer` is gone from `static/counter.js`: it does nothing on a script that code adds (such a script is async), and the tests now assert that the element does not say it and, in a browser, that it is async as added. `scripts/release-build.py` takes `--google-verification` (and `build_release` a `verification`) so that the exact count of the files of the release's site takes the Search Console name from the same two places as the site build (the option, else `$TRIOFORGE_GOOGLE_VERIFICATION`); the count was off by one for a build that was given the option. A `--noindex` preview still takes the file name from the environment (the order named the token).
- 2026-11-21 Part Q: the last round, from the Opus review of Parts N, P and L and from the public CI log. **Q3, the Trusted Types record (review finding 2, probed).** After P6 a script of the page that ran AFTER the site's script and changed a built-in could still widen and redirect the record: `RegExp.prototype.test = () => true` made any address look like an optimizer file, `Set.prototype.has = () => true` made any address look listed, and a `WeakMap.prototype.get` that returned a forged record made the copy loaded later (the optimizer's) mint its worker as the counter's script. Nothing on the way to a minted address in `static/tt-record.js` goes through a prototype now: the shape of an optimizer file is checked by comparing characters, the list of paths is an object with no prototype, the records are two lists scanned by index, loops are by index, and the few functions of the language that are needed are taken once when the file loads; `tests-js/counter.test.mjs` replays the three probes and fourteen more changes of built-ins. WHAT REMAINS, and the header of the file says it: a script that runs BEFORE the site's script can pre-empt the policy's name, take the symbol, or change the built-ins that the file takes at load; nothing in the page can prevent that, and the content security policy limits what such a script can be. **Q4, the query of a counted page (review findings 3 and 4, probed).** The fragment side had an allowlist (an unknown fragment is not counted); the query side had only a test for a parameter named k, and `?k%3D<code>`, `?k%253D<code>`, `?+k=<code>`, `?%20k=<code>`, `?x=1;k=<code>`, `?k[]=<code>`, `?k%00=<code>` and the full-width `?ｋ=<code>` were counted. A page is now counted only when its query is empty or every key of it is one of the site's own switches (`SITE_QUERY_KEYS` in `counter.js`: race, role, tier, setup, buffs, haste, w, p, which a test reads from `trio.js`) and no value says `=`, `&` or `;` however it is encoded; the kit test decodes until the text stops changing (the old limit of three rounds let `#k%2525253D<code>` pass it, which the fragment allowlist caught). **A link that another site added a parameter to (`utm_source` and the like) is not counted** (R1, in the Part R entry, makes that true on a trio page whose switch is malformed too: the address it was opened with is judged). That is the decision: a campaign tag is not one of the site's switches, and nothing known to be clean is the rule; the cost is that visits that arrive through such links are missing from the counts, and the About page says so (the sentence that the counted address may carry a tracking parameter is gone). The history of the tab is held to the same function (`entryMayStay`: no kit in any spelling and a query of the site's own keys; the fragment of an entry of another page is only held to holding no kit, since the ids of that page are not known): the entries of a tab with an earlier page that carried a foreign parameter keep the later pages of that tab from being counted. Addresses written before there was one evidence level carry `ev=`, which nothing reads: they are not counted. **Q7, the never-committed scan (review finding 7, probed).** P5's scan missed: UTF-32 (a UTF-32 little-endian byte order mark begins `FF FE`, which was decoded as UTF-16 and hid the run), a text file with one stray NUL (taken for binary and not read), and UTF-16 text that is mostly non-ASCII (too few NUL bytes to look like UTF-16). `found_in_tracked_text` now reads every file as its raw bytes (Latin-1: any run of the ASCII digits of a token is a run, whatever else the file holds) and as UTF-16 and UTF-32 of either endianness, and never skips a file as binary; the repository itself is scanned in about as long as before and finds nothing. STILL NOT COVERED, and a test holds the list: a token split over lines, written in base64, with `\u` escapes or percent-encoded, or standing inside a longer run of hex digits (a hash of another kind: the scan looks for runs of exactly 32, so that a sha-256 is not an alarm); and the history and the commit messages, which the privacy scan's hook reads. **Q8, what the About page says the site keeps (review finding 9).** The page said "three things" and that session storage is cleared when the tab closes. A kit opened from a shared link is kept in the tab's history entry (`history.state`, by `input/kitlocation.js`; it survives a reload and a restored session) and was left out; and a browser that restores its tabs may restore session storage. Both builds now say four things (the fourth: that kit, kept in that tab's history entry so that a reload shows it again, gone when the entry is) and that the flag of the optimizer is kept "for the browser session". The test that pins the page to the code also holds every call of `pushState` or `replaceState` of the page code to a table (the kit in `kitlocation.js`; `trio.js` passes the state it finds through) and the regular expression of storage access sees `win["localStorage"]`, `store["setItem"]` and `window.name`. **Q2, the Python job of CI is a matrix of five shards (review finding 8).** With the tests that need Node (CI skipped them until Part L) one job could not finish inside the 30 minutes that the hardening test allows (it ran 18 minutes on GitHub before they were added; the whole suite is an hour on the maintainer's computer, the Node files about 13 minutes of it). The timeout stays at the cap; the suite is cut by test FILE (`pipeline/tests/shards.py`, `python -m pytest --shard I/N`, an option of `pipeline/tests/conftest.py`): a file is never split (its module-scoped site build or precompute is made once and is most of its time), 23 heavy files are placed by hand by their measured seconds (longest first, into the shard that has the least), every other file goes by a hash of its name, so a new test file lands somewhere without anyone choosing it. From the measured seconds of a whole run (this computer, busy with other work) the shards are 15.7, 13.3, 12.9, 14.2 and 13.8 minutes; `test_release_build.py` alone is 11.5 minutes and cannot be cut. Each cell sets Node and the packages up the same way and requires Node; `fail-fast` is off, so a run shows every failure. `pipeline/tests/test_shards.py` holds that the shards together cover every test file exactly once, that none is empty, that the option keeps exactly the files of its shard, that the heavy files are spread, and that the workflow's matrix is the one the code has. ONLY A REAL RUN ON GITHUB PROVES the time of a shard: the 18 minutes of the old job (without the Node files) suggest that a runner is faster than this computer, which would make each shard 5 to 10 minutes; if it is as slow, a shard is about 15, and `SHARDS` and the table of heavy files are the two places to change. **Q1, the public CI was red on 19 tests (two causes).** (A) The tests give a Windows path with the platform "win32"; on a Linux runner that is a relative name, which lies in the checkout, and the work-tree guard of `checks_dir` / `checksDir` refused it (6 Python tests, 2 smoke-live tests); the guard is about folders of THIS computer and is now asked only of a path of this computer's own kind (a path of another kind is resolved by the rules of its own platform and not asked about); a path that is really in a work tree is still refused on both platforms. (B) The repository has release pointers in `releases/`, the smoke check by default expects the data release of the newest, and its list of problems then has a third line; 11 smoke-live tests of `main()` expected two (the same 11 failed on the maintainer's computer and were carried as baseline): they pass `--expect-data none`. **Q5, the weekly check (review finding 5).** A page the check could not read, and a navigation with no content type, are problems, not a pass; the kit link page is opened in a browser context of its own and is looked at three ways (the requests to either host of the counter, listened for until the page is closed; a counter script element in the DOM; the resource timing). **Q6, the pin of the fragment links** also reads the links that Python builds (`view.py`: `/sources/#races-box`) and checks each id against the page its path names.
- 2026-11-21 Part R: the closing round, from the Opus review of Part Q. **R1, the address the document was loaded with (review finding 1, traced through the code).** Q4 held the counted address to the site's own query keys, but `trio.js` rewrites the address at start-up (`history.replaceState`, keeping only its own keys) when a switch is malformed, and the counter is added after that: `/trio/<slug>/?race=zzz&utm_source=x`, `?tier=99&fbclid=abc`, `?race=zzz&ev=strict` and `?race=zzz&k=<payload>` looked clean and were counted, while the Performance navigation entry still held the payload. `first.js`, the first module of the page script, now keeps `location.href` as it was in a constant (`loadedAddress`) that no later script can write; `app.js` hands it to `addCounter`, which counts a page only when `mayBeCounted` holds for that copy AND for the current address (a missing copy: not counted), and the Performance navigation entry, where the browser has one, is a second witness. So what the Q4 paragraph above says (a foreign parameter, `ev=`, a kit in the query: not counted) is true on a trio page whose switch is malformed too; `tests-js/counter-loaded.test.mjs` runs the real start-up (first.js, the trio page code, then the counter) for the review's four addresses, and a browser test opens two of them. **R2, the policy is claimed at load (review finding 2, probed).** Q3's guarantee held once the record existed, but the record was made at first use: on a kit page, on every page of a build with no token and on every page the counter refuses, that is the optimizer's first use, long after any other script, and a script in between could take the name `trioforge`, hook `createPolicy` (and then see the site's policy made), or plant a record under the symbol. `tt-record.js` now makes the record and the policy as its own module is evaluated (the page script's first act with Trusted Types), on every page that runs the page script and in both builds; the optimizer's bundle finds the record, and the claim never throws (a browser that will not make the policy, or has none, gives the counter or the optimizer its error when it asks, as before). The 404 page and `boot.js` do not load `tt-record.js` and are unchanged. `getEnginePolicy` no longer copies the list it is given (the copy was an assignment on an array, which a setter on `Array.prototype[0]` could swallow: a denial of the optimizer); the record reads each path once. The header says what remains: a script that runs BEFORE the page script. `tests-js/tt-claim.test.mjs` replays the probes lazy-name, lazy-hook, lazy-symbol and array-setter after the load. **R3, the cost of the decoding and the values of the switches (review findings 3 and 4, probed).** Decoding to a fixed point was quadratic (each round can shorten the text by two characters: 8,000 levels of nesting took 234 ms, 32,000 levels 3.7 s on the visitor's page, from a crafted link of about 1 MB): the decoding is capped at eight rounds (text that is still changing after that is not known to be clean) and an address whose query and fragment together are longer than 4,096 characters is not looked at and not counted. The value of each switch must have the shape that `trio.js` takes for it (`SITE_QUERY_SHAPES`, one table beside `SITE_QUERY_KEYS`, held to `trio.js` and to the baked page by the pin test), so `?w=<payload>`, `?p=<payload>` and `?race=<payload>` are not counted; this replaces Q4's "no `=`, `&` or `;` in a value". **R5, the checks-folder guard sees through links (review findings 5 and 6, probed).** The guard of `checks_dir` / `checksDir` walked the parents of the path by NAME, so a junction or a symbolic link to a subfolder of a checkout (`plain/j` to `repo/build`) was accepted (this was so before Part Q). It is now asked of the path as written AND of its real path (Python `os.path.realpath`, Node `fs.realpathSync.native` of the longest part that exists, the rest kept), on every platform. And the `platform` parameter, which Q1 made the guard's switch, could skip it: on Windows `/x` is on the current drive whatever platform is given; a path with a root is now guarded on Windows as well. What is left, and said: a path of another kind of platform than the computer's that is no path here (a Windows drive path on Linux, a relative name) is returned as written and must not be used as a folder; no caller gives a platform (`checks_common.py` and `smoke_live.mjs` use the process's own), and no environment variable can. **R7, a classic script element, not a module (a decision from the code and the standards: no network, and Cloudflare's script was not fetched).** The source of the module form is the install snippet that Cloudflare's dashboard showed the site owner on 2026-11-21, which loads its script as `<script type='module' ...>`; older snippets, and the tag that this repository describes in Part P and in the tests of the smoke check, are classic scripts with `defer` (both are named here, and neither could be fetched to compare); `counter.js` adds a classic element. A module script is fetched in CORS mode (no cookies go to the other origin, but it does not load unless the host answers with an Access-Control-Allow-Origin header, which cannot be checked from here) and is deferred when the parser inserts it; a classic script is fetched without CORS, and with the visitor's cookies for Cloudflare's host if the browser holds any (a cross-origin classic request has credentials mode `include`, where a module request has `same-origin` and sends none; the site sets no cookie for that host and has no way to, so what could be sent is only what the browser already holds for it from other sites: a small reason for the module form, or for `crossorigin="anonymous"`, which would also need the CORS header), and a script that the page script adds by code is asynchronous either way, so the deferral buys nothing here, while the Content Security Policy (`script-src` names the host, for both forms) and the Trusted Types path (`createScriptURL`, for the `src` of either) are the same. The classic element is KEPT, because nothing found requires the module form and it cannot fail on a missing CORS header; what it cannot run is a script file that uses `import`, `export` or `import.meta`, or one that relies on a module's top-level `this` or scope, which the first-deploy check shows at once (the console has an error and no request goes to `cloudflareinsights.com/cdn-cgi/rum`: the runbook says to confirm both), and the change is then one line, `script.type = "module"` in `counter.js`, with the policy and the headers holding as they are; the test that pins the classic element (`tests-js/counter.test.mjs`, "adds a classic script element") changes with it, which is what makes the change a decision. Not known, and not assumed: whether the current script finds its own tag by `document.currentScript` (a module has none).
- 2026-11-21 Part S: the last small round, from the Opus review of Part R (accept with fixes, nothing major). **S1, the optimizer's copy finds the record without calling a function of the language (review finding 1, probed).** R2 made the page script claim the policy as it loads, but the optimizer's chunk carries another copy of `tt-record.js`, evaluated late, and that copy looked the record up under `Symbol.for("trioforge.tt-policy")`, taken at its own load: a script that ran between the two swapped `Symbol.for` for a function that returned a symbol of its own, with a forged record under it, and the late copy was handed that record, which minted the optimizer's worker as the counter's script (the Q3 outcome again, and against the header). The record is now shared under a string-named property, `window["trioforge.tt-policy"]`, defined not writable, not configurable and not enumerable at the claim, and the late copy reads it by plain property syntax as the first thing it does, before any call (`typeof` and a property read cannot be swapped by a later script; `Symbol.for`, `Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor` and the rest can). The object is `window` and not `globalThis` because a later classic script can shadow `globalThis` (`let globalThis = ...` is allowed: the property is writable and configurable, checked with node's `vm`, and every module evaluated afterwards reads the other object, which is the same hole by another door), while `window` is a non-configurable property of the page's global object, which a later `let window` cannot shadow (the HTML standard's rule for it; not run in a browser here); a worker or a test with no `window` uses `globalThis`. A value under the name that is not the record (an element with that id makes a named property of the window, and an object can be inherited) is not taken for it: the claim makes its own as an own property, which shadows it, and the claim takes the name first on every page, whatever Trusted Types the browser has. What a later script CAN still do, and the header of `tt-record.js` now says in place of "cannot be replaced": deny the optimizer its worker, by replacing `window.trustedTypes` before the late copy loads (the copy makes a record on that object, its policy gives back plain strings and the browser refuses them at a Worker) or by changing the array iterator that `tt-policy.js` lists the optimizer's paths through (the call throws); neither widens what the policy mints, and the real record and policy stay as they were. Tests (`tests-js/tt-claim.test.mjs`): the review's symbol-for probe as a test (it failed on the code as it was: the optimizer was handed the counter's address), a swapped `Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor` and a throwing `Symbol.for`, the two denial routes with the outcome the header states, the shadowed `globalThis` and the planted non-record values in a context of their own (`vm`); with `globalThis` in place of `window`, or a bare `!== undefined` read, the matching test fails. **S2, a part that ends in a dot or a space is refused in both languages (review finding 2, probed).** R5 asked the guard of the real path, and the two real paths of a name written `j.` differ on Windows: Python's `os.path.realpath` goes through Win32 normalisation, which drops the dot, so `<plain>\j.\x` with `j` a junction to `repo\build` was the folder `repo\build\x` and was refused; Node opens the path with the `\\?\` prefix, where `j.` is no folder (`existsSync` is false, `realpathSync.native` gives ENOENT), so `realPathOf` stopped at `plain` and the value was accepted, and the stamps would have been written to a folder that is literally named `j.`. The claim of one rule in Python and in Node was false for that spelling. Now, on Windows (the platform that is given is `win32`, or the computer is one: a rooted `/x` is read by Windows whatever the platform that was given), a value with a part that ends in a dot or a space is refused by its spelling, before any real path is asked, in `checks_dir` and in `checksDir` alike (the parts `.` and `..` and a dot inside a name are fine; the parts are cut at `/` and `\`). Where the computer is not a Windows one a dot at the end is a name like any other. The loosened assertion of R5 in `test_checks_dir.py` (`asked[:1]`) was not needed (the stub answers True, so the guard stops at the first question): it is the full list again. **S3, the check of the current address in `addCounter` has a test of its own (review finding 3, reasoned and then shown by mutation).** After R1 every call of `addCounter` in the tests passed `loaded: window.location.href`, the same address as the current one, and `mayBeCounted` is deterministic, so the two checks always agreed and the one on the current address (`counter.js`, `!mayBeCounted(win.location.href, doc)`) could be deleted with every vitest still green. `counter-loaded.test.mjs` now has the other order: first.js loads a clean address, the page is then moved to one that carries a campaign parameter, a click identifier, a kit as `k=` or as `#k=`, or a malformed switch, and nothing is added (with a control that two clean addresses are counted); with that one condition deleted the new test fails and nothing else does, and it is back. The test helper takes an optional `now` for the move. **S4, the pin of the value shapes can no longer drift in silence (review finding 4, probed).** The shapes of R3 are a little wider than what `trio.js` takes (`tier=99`, `role=abcdefghijklmnop`, `race=QWE` are counted: the table is a gate against kit codes and foreign parameters, not a second copy of the validation, and it fails closed), but the test that holds the table to the page captured the values of the baked page with patterns that were themselves subsets of the shapes (`[A-Z]{3}`, `[a-z]+`), so a new role, buffs or race code with a digit or a dash was simply not captured, and the test passed while those pages were never counted; the fixture also has two of the six roles. The pin now captures the WHOLE value of each radio and option (`value="([^"]+)"`), and takes every role and setup code from `pipeline/trioforge/site/data.py` (`ROLES`, `SETUPS`) and every race code from `rules/races.json` as well, and each must fit its shape: with `healer2` added to the roles, or `Bow-2` to the setups, the test fails (shown once for each, and put back). A tier is still `\d{1,2}` and a build's switch letters are as before. **S5, text, and one line of code (review notes 5, 6 and 7).** The R7 paragraph above now says where the module form comes from (the dashboard's snippet of 2026-11-21, with the older classic `defer` snippets named), that a classic cross-origin request may carry the visitor's cookies for Cloudflare's host where a module request carries none (the site sets none there), and that the test which pins the classic element changes with the one line, in place of a sentence that said that the tests would hold. The first-deploy check of the runbook asks for at least one request to `cloudflareinsights.com/cdn-cgi/rum` and not exactly one (what the real script sends, and how often, was never seen: it may send a second report, for web vitals say, and a false alarm there would send the reader looking for a fault that is not one), and says to write down the count that was seen, which step (2) then holds the later requests to. `smoke_live.mjs` no longer swallows a failed send-away of the kit link page to `about:blank`: it is one line of the list of problems, `could not be sent away to about:blank (...), so a report that the page sends as it goes may be missed and this check shows less than it should`, since pagehide may not have fired and the check would pass on less than it claims. The changelog's 2026-11-21 entry is after the last of 2026-11-20, in date order. And the comment of `first.js` said that `loadedAddress` is "the first statement of the first module": the first module to be evaluated is `input/kitlocation.js`, which `first.js` imports and which has no top-level effect (it only defines constants and functions), so the comment now says that, and the claim that matters is unchanged (nothing of the page script that can touch the address runs before the constant is read).
- 2026-11-22 Part T: the scoring pass from the game client's own formulas. **The rule that kept them out is lifted (the owner's decision of 2026-11-22: the risk to the game's terms is accepted, the sources of the game's own files need no attribution, and what counts is the best product with accurate data).** A formula read from the game client's own calculation may be used in `rules/rules.json`, the model, the browser engine and the site. What stays: the eqlwiki CC BY-SA attribution and share-alike, and every privacy rule (no game logs, no character names, no text taken from chat). A rule that comes from the client's calculation has a source of the new kind `game-client`, which carries one fixed label ("EverQuest Legends client, build of 2026-11-17: the client's own calculation"; the schema still refuses an address, an offset, a program-file name or a listing, so none of them is in a file) and the seal `measured`, with any public source or reading of the owner that also supports the rule kept beside it; the Rules page says once, in a plain sentence, that some rules now come from the client's own calculations. The version of the rules file stays `2026-11-19` (nothing was released under it: the content hash that a test pins beside it moves, as the order of that test says). **T1, item attack does not tier (the game client's own calculation).** The client never scales the attack of an item (nor its heal amount, spell damage, clairvoyance or delay): a +4 or a +10 item's attack is its +0 value, where the model gave it the stat rule (10 became 14 at +4 and 20 at +10). A new rule, `tier.unscaled_stats` (`["ATK"]`), lists the stats the stat rule leaves alone (the model carries attack only); `at_tier` in Python and `atTier` in the browser engine read it (from `tier_rules.unscaled_stats` of the bundle) and refuse a value that is not a list of stats of the model; the two are held to each other by the parity fixtures. `tier.stat_rule` keeps its wiki source and gains the client's, since the client agrees with its numbers at whole tiers. The default kits of the 31 sweep trios at +4 and +10 (180 of them each time) moved by item as follows: T1 none, T2 2, T3 29, T4 14, T5 17, T6 5, T7 none, T8 7, T9 none, and T10 changes words and no score. **T2, the armour class soft cap (the game client's own calculation).** The client's cap is the larger of 350 and the best class cap at the level (`Resources/ACMitigation.txt`), before Combat Stability and Physical Enhancement add their percent, and what counts past it is the larger of 0.3 and the best rate of the trio's classes, which can be another class than the one with the best cap. The model took the best class cap and the rate of that same class: a trio of casters (best cap 328, rate 0.25) had a cap of 360 with Combat Stability 3 and now has 385 (367 and 392 with Physical Enhancement as well, which a trio of casters is not given by default), and a trio whose best rate is 0.25 or 0.28 (a caster, a Shaman, a Rogue, a Beastlord, a Berserker) reads 0.3. Two rules hold the floors, `ac.softcap.base_floor` (350) and `ac.softcap.rate_floor` (0.3); `make_profile` reads them (so they are in every pick), and `ac.softcap.past_cap_rate` says in its value that the rate is the best of the trio's classes and at least the floor. The browser engine reads the cap and the rate from the profile of the bundle, so it needed no change of its own; the fixtures that a trio of casters reaches were written again. **T3, the Monk worn-weight bonus and the Iksar racial armour class sit inside the sheet value, before the cap (the game client's own calculation).** The client adds both to X before the soft cap, so past the cap they count at the rate past it (a third), not in full: for a Monk trio that is the largest single change of the pass, since the worn weight of the kit then matters far less and heavy armour with a high armour class gains. The model already had the placement (`placement` `inside` of the profile, in the evaluator, the builder and the browser engine): the rules `monk.bonus_placement` and `race.iksar_ac_placement` now say `inside X before the cap` (sealed measured, from the client's calculation, with the maintainer's reading of the Iksar beside the second), the old reading is the alternative of the first (the robustness pass flips to it, and its variants are now `Monk placement top` and `Iksar placement top`, with no in-game test to name, so a flip note reads `Iksar, if Iksar AC stacks on top of the cap`), and neither rule is open any more (the thresholds of the Monk bonus, `monk.weight_curve`, still are). Python and the browser engine needed no code of their own: the placement was a field of the profile that both read. **T4, the resist cap is 1000 a school (the game client's own calculation).** The stat window of the client caps each resist at 1000 up to level 65 (and abilities of the Discordant Defiance kind raise it); the model counted the gear and buff resists of a school up to 200, a number of its own. `resist.cap` is 1000 now, sealed measured from the client's calculation, `STANDARD_CAPS` of the evaluator follows it, and the profile, the builder and the browser engine read it from the profile as before. What the cap did before was to stop a resist item from counting past 200 a school; at 1000 no kit comes near it (the resists that gear and buffs give a school are tens, a few hundred at the most), so the resist term is now linear in the resists a kit wears, at the weights of the builds: the Shield caster's 1.5 and the Caster tank's 0.8 a point of each school. No weight was changed. The fixtures that put kits at the cap (the evaluate terms, the brute-force instances) are made at the new cap, and the resist-capped stratum of the evaluate family, which no kit of the public catalog reaches any more, is among the rare ones with the regen cap. The new cap moved 14 default kits of the sweep (11 of the Shield caster, 2 of the Caster and 1 of the Caster tank). **T5, the attribute knees (the game client's own calculation).** The only break at 255 in the client is the one of stamina into hit points (half a point a point above it, which stays in `hp.sta_curve`); strength, agility, wisdom, intelligence, charisma and dexterity have none, and the model's own knee was on dexterity alone, where it valued a point past 255 at a tenth. `stats.dex_post255_slope` is 1 now, so dexterity counts in full up to 510 (the Archer's weight of 1.6 and the Melee build's of 0.8 now see the dexterity of gear past 255), and `stats.knee` keeps its 255 for stamina and says in its notes what holds for each stat: its title is now `Stamina knee (into hit points)`, its seal measured with the client's calculation beside the EQL Tools page, and its private source is gone because its notes now name numbers. The evaluator, the builder (`DEX_PAST_KNEE`) and the browser engine (`dexPastKnee`) carry the constant, which the bundle exports and the tests compare; what the game server does with dexterity (weapon procs) is not known and is not scored. **T6, negative stats shrink with the tier (the game client's own calculation).** The client moves a negative stat toward zero by the amount a positive stat of the same size would grow, `max(n, floor(|base| x n / 10))`, and never past zero (-2 is -1 at +1 and 0 at +2, -20 is -12 at +4), and does so for the attributes, the resists, hit points, mana, endurance and armour class alike; the model left negatives where they were. `tier.negatives_scale`, which was a flag that said false and was open (test B6), is the rule's own text now (`toward zero by max(n, floor(|base|*n/10)), never past zero`, sealed measured), `at_tier` and `atTier` implement it and refuse any other value, the bundle carries the text, and a stat that the tiers shrink to nothing is dropped like any zero stat. A test holds the arithmetic to the client's own (float32, `base + (int)min(-b, max(t, -0.1 t b))`) for every base from -400 to 400 at every whole tier. One effect on the fixtures: with negatives scaled, a bracer and a ring of the invented-session sample each gained exactly 2 armour class and 2 resist from +0 to +2, and two kits of the same exact value and the same total tie rank (the tie stage takes the least total rank of the `(key, tier)` order, which two such swaps leave equal) were found by the two engines in different arrangements, in upgrade-009 of the parity sample. The first answer drew that session's owned items again (`REROLLED` in `fixture_cases.py`); the repair round of Part T (the entry below) took that back, made the tie a total order in both engines, and upgrade-009 is its original draw. **T7, the haste cap has a level dimension (the game client's own calculation).** The client's cap on the total haste is `max(level, 30) + 25` up to level 50 (75 at 50), 85 for levels 51 to 59 and 100 from level 60, plus the overhaste that Unbound Alacrity is taken to be (it lifts the cap by what it adds, so 85 at level 50 for a trio with a Monk, which the rule `haste.unbound_alacrity` already said). The model had 75 and 85 and no level in it. `haste.cap_pct` and `haste.cap_pct_with_monk` now state the formula as text (the model implements those texts and refuses any other), `formulas.haste_cap_at(level)` computes it, `make_profile` puts the cap of the level (plus the 10 with a Monk) in the profile and the bundle exports it, and the browser engine reads the cap from the profile as before. At level 50 nothing changes (a test holds 75 and 85, and the cap of level 50 is the same by construction; the sweep of T7, the 31 trios at +4 and +10, found 0 of 180 default kits moved, and the later items were measured against it); at level 55, the level of the next tables, the cap is 85 (95 with a Monk), so a haste spell of 60 percent leaves 25 points for worn haste and not 15. The second source of haste the client has (a bard's song of the second kind, up to 10 more at level 50) is not modelled: the model takes the best spell haste and the Monk's 10, as before (held). **T8, the Berserker stance for the Melee build of a trio with a Berserker (the game client's own calculation).** In the stance the client divides the swing timer by 2.1 after the haste cap (the haste is capped as before, with or without a haste spell, and the factor comes on top of whatever haste counts) and takes agility out of mitigation and evasion (while endurance is above one percent, which the model takes to be kept up). The new rule `stance.berserker` holds the factor, whether agility counts and the list of builds that play the stance (the Melee damage build); `make_profile` gives the profile a `stance` (None for every other build and for a trio with no Berserker), the evaluator multiplies the swing of the two hands by the factor (not a bow, not a weapon proc) and counts none of the agility in the sheet and the evasion, the builder, the pruning and the brute-force twin do the same, and the bundle carries the stance on the role. The factor is exact (2.1); the client rounds the timer to a whole number of hundredths of a second, which the model does not. The trio page says the stance in plain words under Weapons and in the folded Assumptions line when the page opens on a build that plays it, and the page code says it when the visitor picks one. No weight of the build is tuned: the stance is a rule of the game, and the weights keep their values (a Melee build now values a weapon more against stats, as the game does). Not modelled, held: the Rogue and Beastlord agility bands of mitigation, which the model has no bands for. **T9, the damage bonus of a main hand with none listed has the level in it (the game client's own calculation).** The client's bonus is `(level / 100) x max(damage, level) x f x min(delay, 50) / 40` from level 28 on, with f 0.8 for a one-hander and 1.1 for a two-hander. At level 50 this is what the model had for a one-hander whose damage is at most 50 (half the delay), and 0.6875 x min(delay, 50) for a two-hander where the model had 0.67; it differs for a weapon with more damage than the level (its own damage counts), for a one-hander slower than 50 (the delay counts at most 50 for both) and below level 28 (none). The new rule `weapons.fallback_bonus` states it, `weapons.py` and the evaluator each write the formula out, and the builder, the pruning, the brute-force twin and the browser engine pass the level of the profile. A listed bonus is used as it is, a listed zero counts as none, the off hand has none and a bow has none, as before. The client rounds the bonus down when it shows it; the model keeps the exact value, as it always did (the formula is the item window's figure, and the server applies its own), which stays open. **T10, the haste is presented as two answers with the reason (owner decision).** For a trio that has a haste spell among the buffs of the view, the trio page says in plain words, beside the haste figure of the totals and beside the box "I have a haste spell": without a spell the best worn haste counts in full up to the cap; with the trio's spell (named, with its percent, from the buff table of the bundle: the best haste of the buffs of the choice) only the cap minus the spell counts (the numbers are given, at level 50 75 minus the spell, and with Unbound Alacrity's flat 10 said for a Monk trio), so a smaller haste item reaches the cap and its slot goes to stats. The baked page keeps the gear-haste kit, as before, and ticking the box solves the kit with the spell in the browser, as before. The kit with the spell is not named at build time: it would need a second full solve of every view of every trio (the grid holds none for it), so the page says "tick the box to see that kit"; once the box is ticked the notes say the kit below is chosen with the spell. The words are written by `haste_notes` in `view.py` into the baked page and by `hasteNotes` in `trio.js` when the buffs change, and a test holds the two to each other. A choice of buffs with no haste spell keeps the plain note of the row and has no note beside the box.
- 2026-11-23 Part T, the repair round (after two reviews of Part T; the commits on top of c393f8d, nothing pushed or released; no weight was changed, and no rules entry but the words of one note). **R1 (93819fd).** A comment put in the middle of an assert had cut off `caps["regen"] == {...}` in the shard test of `test_precompute.py`, so the equality was no longer asserted; it is again. Every comment added to the test files of f300595..HEAD and every rewritten assert (against the line it replaced) were checked for the same mistake: there is no other. **R2, a total order for the last tie (b8cd642, a780a35, a50e034, fdebc40).** The reroll of upgrade-009 (`REROLLED`, T6) had hidden a real disagreement of the two engines. With the original draw the session holds two kits of the same exact value and the same total tie rank, {w33397@2, w49006@0} and {w33397@0, w49006@2} (two items that each gain exactly the same from +0 to +2 and stand next to each other in the sorted pool, so 1 + 4 = 2 + 3 in rank), and Python's solver named the first, the browser engine the second. Now the tie stage is followed by a last stage in both engines (`_settle_ties` in `solver/solve.py`, `settleTies` in `static/engine/solve.js`; the head of each file states it). **The order:** among the kits of the exact value of the best and the least total rank, the answer is the kit that is first when the canonical kits are compared place by place in the order of the 22 places (an item before an empty place, two items by key as text and then tier, the first place that differs deciding) and, with every place alike, the chosen buff ids as sorted lists, the lower list first; a kit of a higher exact value, or of the same value and a lower rank, takes the answer first. It is an order of kits, not of the paths of solvers. **How:** once the tie stage has a kit of the least total rank R, a program is solved that maximizes the value over the kits with the rows `total rank <= R` and "not the worn items (copies counted) and chosen buffs of the kits found so far", with the cutoff of the first stage's kind at 1e-8 relative under the best value (`TIE_PROOF_TOLERANCE`, a tenth of the tie stage's band, ten times the solver's accuracy: a kit that ties has the best value exactly and so is never cut off); every kit it finds that has the best value is a member, is left out of the next program, and the stage ends when a program has no kit worth the cutoff (infeasible, or optimal with a kit under the cutoff, as the first stage's cutoff runs end). A solve of the stage that stops at its limit, or a tie of more than 200 kits (`MAX_TIED_KITS`), leaves `tie_stage` "applied" with `canonical` false, and a canonical precompute refuses such a kit (`grid._solve_one`). **Cost, measured:** with the rank as the objective and the tie stage's band the proof took 5.5 s against 0.8 s for the tie stage and 0.7 s for the first stage on the 8 solves of the Bard/Rogue/Beastlord melee build at +4 and +10, which would have made the precompute some 2.4 times slower; the value as the objective with the cutoff takes 0.9 s there (4.0 s for the 21 solves of the Paladin/Monk/Berserker trio, against 7.4 s of first stages and 7.9 s of tie stages), and the sweep of the 31 trios took 2,191 s against 2,255 s before (the difference is noise); the engine's parity and tie-shortcut test files take 158 s against 128 s. **Effect on kits:** none that was found. The 31 sweep trios at +4 and +10 (wiki pool, own buffs) were solved again with the new stage: 0 of 180 default kits moved, and none of the 486 rows (every setup of every build) differs from the sweep before it in kit, buffs or value; in the parity sample only upgrade-009 changed (its Python kit is the engine's now). The tests: new in `test_solve.py` and `tests-js/engine-tie-order.test.mjs` (the instance of the tie under four namings and shuffles, the buff tie {1, 4} against {2, 3}, the cap, a solve that does not finish, the allowance of each solve, a brute force of the whole order on random instances with twins in Python and twins of the sample in JavaScript, the comparison itself), `test_precompute.py` (the refusal); six of the eight new Python cases of `test_solve.py`, the refusal of `test_precompute.py` and seven of the ten new JavaScript tests failed before the stage. Six existing tests count the solves of a canonical call and were edited because a solve was added (the time limit of each solve in `test_solve.py`; the limits and the solver options of the proof in `engine-tie-shortcut.test.mjs`; the limits of a canonical call in `engine-path.test.mjs` and `engine-solve.test.mjs`). **Of equal additions, one rule for both engines (R14, 8d8a371).** The original draw of upgrade-009 had left two existing tests failing, for a reason in the data and not in the tie-break: at step 1 of that session two acquisitions (w33397 and w49006 to +2) are worth exactly the same, the canonical path took w49006 (the first in the order of kits) and the browser's path, which runs no tie stage, took the other one, after which the two paths differed (its second step was worth 268,883 and not 266,583), so the best set of two additions was not the path's first two steps and the browser's steps did not agree with the recording. Neither test was edited. **The rule:** of the additions of exactly the best value a step takes the first by `(key, tier)` (one candidate to a key), in the canonical path and in the browser's alike. **How:** after the solve of a step a look solves the same program again with only the candidates before the addition found on offer (the others held at none in `owned_caps`, the pool unchanged, so the browser's model is changed in place), again and again while its best value is the step's, and the step's kit is the solve of the first addition alone (canonical when the path is); `_earlier_equal` in `upgrade.py`, `earlierEqual` in `path.js`. **Cost in the browser, measured:** the look asks the solve for a kit worth at least the step's value (`floor` in `solveScenario`: the levels whose relaxation cannot reach it are left out, the others run with a cutoff, and no kit worth it ends the call as proven infeasible), which is what nearly every look finds. On the four Upgrade cases of the sample (eight steps, the phone-bench harness, the best of two runs) the path took 3,042 ms before, 6,448 ms with a look that searched for the best kit, and 3,969 ms with the floor (1.30 times); the first step arrives in 15 to 386 ms before and 24 to 498 ms after. **Effect:** only upgrade-009 changed in the parity sample (its first two steps are w33397@2 twice, then w49006@2, and the best set of two is the path's first two steps); the two tests pass unedited. The tests: new in `test_upgrade.py`, `test_export_fixtures.py`, `engine-path.test.mjs` and `haste-truthful.test.mjs` (six: four failed before the change, the other two, that an earlier addition worth a hair less is not taken and that the first of the equal additions may be a new item or a copy, held already and guard the rule's reach), and two existing tests of the path were edited for the extra solve of a step (the limits and the run to cut short in `engine-path.test.mjs`, the scenarios the session keeps, the budget of the stepper's test that spends it step by step). **R3 (1484161).** `EvalProfile` put the Monk bonus and the Iksar racial armour class on top of the capped sheet value when it was given no placement, which the rules no longer say: its default is "inside" now (`profile.placements` reads the rules, and a test holds the default to them), the exporter's brute-force profile takes the placement from the rules (it wrote "top" in; the random instances draw both), the three invented sessions of the parity sample that use that profile were made again, and the stale texts (formulas.py, the table of the robustness pass, the doc comment of `fallbackBonus` in the engine) say what is true. Five existing tests relied on the old default and now name the placement they test. **R4 (a603635).** The bundle wrote the cap of a trio with a Monk as the cap of the level plus a literal 10; it takes both caps from `profile.haste_caps`, the function the profile's haste cap is made by, so that a changed amount cannot make the bundle disagree with the profile. **R5 (d6fa853).** The Berserker stance sentence said that worn haste "keeps its full value", which the haste note beside it contradicts when a spell is up (only the cap minus the spell counts of worn haste); the model is the note's. The sentence says that the swing is 2.1 times as fast, applied after the haste cap and on top of whatever haste counts. There is one copy of the sentence (`stance_sentence` in `view.py`): the page code only shows or hides the baked note, so there is no second text to keep equal. **R6 (45bbda8).** The Part T entry named a private document of the owner's and its item numbers, and a comment of `test_items.py` did; they say "the game client's own calculation" now (the B numbers that remain are the in-game test ids of the rules). **R8 (530294b).** The note of `ac.softcap.base_floor`, the changelog and the T2 paragraph said a trio of casters has a soft cap of 392 and not 367 at level 50; the model gives 385 (it was 360), because a trio of casters is not given Physical Enhancement (`ac.softcap.pe_for_caster_only_trios` is false), and 392 (367) with it. The three texts say that, a test holds them to the model, and the pinned hash of the rules file moved with the note (the version stays). **R9 (abfd938).** The brute force of the solver tests (`solver_helpers.py`) had not followed Part T: its soft cap had no floor and took the rate of the class with the best cap (T2), its weapon index took no level (T9) and no stance swing (T8), and its sheet armor class and evasion counted the agility the stance takes away (T8); both generators drew the haste on offer from the cap of level 50 (`85 if monk else 75`) at level 55 too (T7). They follow the rules now (three tests failed first), the solver-vs-brute tests pass on the new instances (a new one gives 80 instances the Berserker stance: no defect of the solver was found), and two brute-force cases of the parity sample were made again. **R10, R11, R12 (da1f761, 23bbfbb).** With the haste box ticked the notes said the kit below is chosen with the spell while the page still showed the kit without it: they say it is still the one without the spell until the solved kit is drawn, and after a failed solve; the stance sentence follows the stance's share of agility and has a second sentence for the bow setup (the factor is the swing of the hands); the haste words no longer print "75 minus 80" or "1 points", and the box note of a Monk trio says the flat 10. **R13, the page budget.** The fixture's trio page is 56,958 bytes (55,617 at f300595, 56,750 at c393f8d), 10,626 under `build.LIMITS["trio_html"]` (67,584) and 13 under the `MARKUP_BEFORE` of `test_site_priorities.py`; no limit was raised, and the bow note of R11 was cut to a sentence with no link and no figure to stay inside it. The only trio with complete results here is the fixture's, so the largest real trio page cannot be built locally: the words of Part T put 742 bytes (the two haste notes) on every page of a trio that has a haste spell and 439 more on a trio with a Berserker, so a real page that had some 500 bytes under the limit at f300595 (the 170 of the docstring of `test_site_priorities.py` before the pages lost some 330) would be over it, and the build refuses a page over the limit; this is for the release to settle, with the real pages. **R6 (45bbda8, 2713d5d).** It also takes the two references to a private report and the quoted sentence out of this file. **R7.** A line that said T7 had not been measured was wrong, and another that said it had been was right: the sweep of the T7 tree (31 trios, +4 and +10) found 0 of 180 default kits moved against T6, and the later items were measured against it (the stored results were compared again in this round with the same answer). The T7 paragraph above says that; the cap of level 50 is the same by construction and a test holds 75 and 85.
- 2026-11-24 Nektropos Castle is no longer an `exists_unflagged_zones` entry of `rules/hand-lists.json` (Part U repair round; the maintainers' decision). **Why.** The client flagged the zone (id 28) with the zone names of 2026-11-09 (`derived/client/zones.json`, flagged 78 -> 80: Nektropos Castle and the new Permafrost Keep), so the entry's claim, that the zone exists although the client does not flag it, was no longer true, and `test_committed_hand_list_ids_exist_in_the_client_extract` said so. **What.** The entry is removed and the note at the top of the file says plainly that the client flagged the zone with the zone names of 2026-11-09 and that the entry was removed on 2026-11-24. The catalog was built again offline from the caches of 2026-11-24 (no fetch): the 46 items of the zone page of EQ Legends Tools are still in it with the same stats, drops and classes (they are still read from that site with `--eqlegendstools`, which overrides the wiki's copy as before), the strict count is 0, and no item's evidence level moved. The only difference is evidence text: 61 items (the 46 and 15 wiki items that drop in the zone) say "Drops in Nektropos Castle, a zone the client marks as part of EQL." with the seal client, where they said "a zone that exists in EQL although the client does not flag it. Source: <zone page>." with the seal player; the sentence is written by `evidence.py` from the zone's status, so no code changed and the wording of The Arena and the Tutorial, which the client still does not flag, is as it was. The `source-eql-tools` reason (seal player) stays on the 46. A test of the committed catalog holds all of it (`test_the_committed_catalog_keeps_the_tools_items_and_none_of_its_reasons_says_the_client_does_not_flag_their_zone`: it failed before the catalog was built again). **The release check (Part U, repair round 2).** `release.check_eqlegendstools_source` no longer depends on the hand list: with no entry naming a page of the Tools site it would have gone silent, so it now refuses a catalog whose provenance names no EQ Legends Tools page among its inputs at all (a catalog built without `--eqlegendstools`, whose items are still the wiki's), with a hand-lists file or without one; an `exists_unflagged_zones` entry whose source is a page of the site keeps its own refusal (that page must be among the inputs). The test that says so was written first and failed (`test_a_catalog_that_names_no_page_of_eql_legends_tools_is_refused_whatever_the_hand_lists_say`); the invented catalogs of the release tests name the zone page, as the committed one does.
